WOMEN'S GHAT.

The Queee's Fête.—As many authors have set out to -write an article, and have ended by producing a book, so the bazaar which was held this week in the grounds of the Royal Hospital, Chelsea, was the development of an idea, which was comparitively unambitious. The object, however,—that of aiding the funds of Sir Wiiliam Treloar's home for cripples at Alton, was one calculated to evoke in a special degree the public apmpathy, and so numerous were the cafes of help from leading women in London society that with the co-operation of Queen Alexandra, the effort assumed the proportions of a Queen's fete on a scale of some magnificence. Among many striking features of the fete visitors were particularly struck by the prate ic It work of the boys who are being taught to earn their OWQ living .by the manufacture of various articles -which are in frequent use. Snch charities, which seels to neutralise the disadvantages against which cripples and other affected people have to contend, are in the highest degree commendable, and merit such cordial support as the bezaar received from all classes of society, whose interest in Sir William Treloar's home and college Was stimulated by the kindly example of the Queen. The Royal Visit to Devon and Co.nwall.—Those of .us who have bad occasion to wish that life acre not so much of a rash, would plohably say that if they had their choice they would- not select such a tour as that which the Prince and Princess of Wales have been taking this week in Devon and Cornwall, but it is evident that the loyal enthusiasm with . which their Royal Highnesses have tete received, has been amply sufficient to compensate for a series of what would appear to be somewhat tiring journees. They have further had the opportunity of sseing some of the must beautifel scenery in the West conetry, and of witnessing some quaint ceremoniee a hich still survive in that part of England. With the thonghtfulness svhich the Prince of Wales has inherited from Queen Victoria, and from his Royal father and' wit:eh is equally an attribute of the Princess, they have afforded the public ample oppter- ttmities of seeing them, special facilities being afforded for the children. It folio N.'s that the Royal visit to Devon and Cornwall will long be remembered by the people, and to the Prince and Princess themselves it srill assureJly yield a harvest of happy memeeies. The Church Pageant.—The pageantry resivel is a little less in evidence than in the past year or two, but great interest attaches to the Church Pageant taking place in the grounds of Fulham Palace from June 10th to 16th. The Palace, with its grounds of some thirty- seven acres, is the official residence of the Bishops of -Leaden, and affords a very appropriate site for this historic open-air display. The pageant is divided into some twenty episodes, giving a striking illustration of the rites nnd customs of the aces since the birth of England's Christianity, and of the chief events in the history of the church subsequent to the Norman Con- quest. One of the most striking scenes is the ono depicting- the return from exile and martyrdom of Thomas A Becket, who played a. conspicuous part in defending the rights of the church and ecclesiastical privilege!, and altogether the Pageant should prove of consideracle educational value, in presenting this interesting story of the thousand years' history of the English_ Church. _

Bride's Promise to Obey—Mr. H. IL Mansfield, M.P for Spalding, is of opinion that the bride's promise "to obey" should be eliminated from the marriage service. Speaking the other day at some festivities at Mold, Flintshire, in connection with the marriage of his eldest son, he said, "it was about time we got rid of this barbaric piece of medievalism, and everyone knew that the promise to obey was not as a rule strictly observed in married' life." This is the more generally accepted view in France and America than is the case as pet in this country. It is proposed to omit the promise "to obey" in the revised French civil marriage service, and in the United States not one bride in fifty makes the declaration of obedience. A marriage con- tract N% hi eh is solemnly averred to have been signed and sealed last week before a Justice of the Peace in the town of Montclair, New Jersey, shows how things are reversed in America. In this the bride limited her bnsband's cigars, his control over his getty cash, his freedom of visiting, and to keep a dog, and he was "never to join any Lodge that prohibits women and then not unless she wishes to join too." The bride. groom whose name ought to be Mr. Job Caudle, is said to have subscribed to these and other stringent con- ditions, and he seems likely to have rather a hen-pecked time in store for him. Domestic Hygiene.—Mr. John Burns, in opening the Tuberculosis Exhibition in London last week, touched upon many matters of domestic hygiene. Light and fresh air are the two great preventives of consumption, and Mr. Burns urged the necessity for open windows, the open 'score register, and also for the abolition ot the unhygienic baby's "comforter." He also had something to say about the "best parlour" in the small home, which he eescribed as a" mausoleum for wax-fruit and china cloy, sacred to the landlord, the insurance man, the doctor, the tax-collector, and the undertaker." Mr. Burns is not the first to point out to the working man the folly of paying rent for ft room -which is used by the family on only a few days of the year, but the " best partour "is hallowed by tradition

and sentiment, and its use as a living room would add to the household work. Pur these reasons it is too much to hope that the working classes will abolish the parlour, and add to the breathirig space of the family, but it is possible for one and all to secure the benefits of the open window niad to refrain from darkening it with curtains shutting out the beautiful light. Children and Sweets.-1/ictors and scientists have iong discarded the old household maxim that sweets and sweet things are bad for children, and leading members of the medical professor recognise the value of buter- scotch in the treatment of tuberculosis, and other wasting diseases. Children cannot be uniformly fed, like cattle. They have their likes and dislikes, and is the case of the vary large number of children who from it reluctance to take certain kinds of food are fed upon a deficient amount of fat, the bony structures aro unperfect and slow of growth. Pure sweetmeats, such as butter-scotch, will so far to male up this deftciency, and especially useful in supplying nutriment in a most ; palatible and easily digested form is the combination of sugar and butter in this old confection.

Gin-earning the Debutante.—For some time past, the debutante's "coming-out" ball gown has ceased to be all-white, pale tones of colour having been chosen instead. Many deplore the piocedute argueing—and no doubt correctly—that it is always possible to Bnd some tone of ivory or cream, that proves becoming to the young wearer. Some time ago, a leading London modizte hinted that, she proposed introducing colohr into several of the trains or gowns of debutantes to be presented at the Court this we ,k, but as a matter of feet this scheme has fallen through entirely. Even thoir bouquets have been white, and those by the way, have taken the form of the "shower," shaped Eke a large ostrich egg, but petfeetly light and transparent. Tho'fbwers most in favour for the debutante have proved to be white orchids, lilies-of-the-valley, and pelargoniums.

Wh a the fan is folded, the blooms form a bouTa?t, smill specimens, closinz up into corsage knots. WARRINGTON'S HEALTH.

MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT DISCUSSED.

Should Warrington Have its Own Coroner?

One of the most important subjects. for discussion at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Town Council on Tuesday, was the annual report of the Medical Officer of Health, a summary of which appeared in last week's " Examiner." When the minutes of the Health Com- Mittee were submitted, Councillor HAWTHORN said he did not think that if members of the Council studied the report they would be well satisfied with the ssnitary coniitiens of the Borough. The mere fact that the death rate bad gone up a little during the year was rather disquieting, considerin,, the efforts they were making to keep it down. Last' week be nes looking at the mortality returns for this part of the country and he was rather surprised to find that War- rington bad the highest death rate of any borough in the district. There was no doubt that Warrington was suffering from the terrible legacies which had been left to it—old buildings, noisome alleys and courts which required demolition—and the progress the Council had made in the work of demolition had not been as rapid as many of them would wish. Only that night he passed a row of houses on Heath-s'de ehieh, to say the least, were an eyesore and not altogether cre litable to the Corporation as sanitary houses. With regard to the defects pointed out by the medical officer, as to the pail closet systna, that was a matter which ought to be taken up at mice. They would not reduce the &alit-rate until they attended to these s.nitary defects and he hoped the chairman of the Health Committee would hurry on those who were a little delinquent in this respect. Alderman BENNETT referred to the horrible condition that some of the slums in the town were allowed to remain in. He WeS glad to find that slow as the progress had been, the Health Committee seemed to be increasingly alive to the necessities of the situation. The medical officer bad put his finger on the weak spots and his opinion seemed to coincide with the report of the official of the National Housing Reform Council. In spite of what bad been done, the conditions were anything but creditable to the Council. lie coald not sit doe's quietly end think of peer little children ding almost like flies in the slums of Warrington without feeling it was the absolute duty of every humane man and patriotic citizen to strain every nerve to find a remedy: While the members of the Council were laus hing, joking and enjoying themselves, these little children were dying day by day and hour by hour. The aldsrman proceeded to discourse upon the smoke nuisance, stating that the llealth Committee appeared to be realizing their obligations in the matter, as a oonsiderable number of observation—though not nearly as many as there ought to have been—had been taken. The. nuisance, however, was still very great and alarming, and the blight was gradually extending even to the environs of the town. He appealed to the manufacturers, in the friendliest spirit, to introduce proper appliances and if possible not only to lessen the dolauk smoke nuisance, but to deal with the noxious gases which were even more injurious. He was afraid that one of his particular models in regard to the question of smoke abatement. was at present not quite carrying out its high reputa- tion in the matter. Alderman Bennett concluded by instancing the Case of Messrs. Bibby of Liverpool, who estimated that by the adoption of improved equipment they wore saving £5,000 a your, as well as doing away with the emission of black smoke. Councillor Dr. JOSEPH said there was something very discouraging in the death rate being higher, but there was one encouraging feature, and that was the mild character of the scarlet fever. He remembered when he came to Warrington, during the first seven years that he was medical officer of health, it was an exceedingly rare thing for them to get mild cases. The majority of them were rralignant, but now-a-days it was just as rare to get malignant cases. That was no doubt due to the improvement in the sanitary condi- tions. He was sorry to say there was a, tendency for diphtheria and typhoid to crop up—and especially diphtheria—and it they could only hit on the right nail to stamp out these most potent, virulent and treacherous diseases of the infectious type; then they do a great deal to lower the death rate. Another very important point was the infantile death rate, and that brought him to consider the uncertified deaths, which were over 4 per cent. Warrington had an unenviable notoriety in the country for that class of deaths. He would like to see that remedied and he had thought about it many times, but he did not •see any cure for it except by having a local coioner. As long as they Lad a coroner living at a distance with a wide district in which he had to hold many inquests during the dey, they could not expect anything better. It was a well-known thing all over the couutryamongst coroners and others associated with inquiries into death that that nothing deterred people who neglected their cbildren— sometimes wilfully and criminally— SQ much f.'s the hAd- ing of an inquest in every case where was anything like a shadow of suspicion. That could never be done, so long as we had not a coroner of our own, and the time had come when a town of the size of Warrington should have a coroaer.

Councillor FULTON SMITH said it seemed to give peculiar satisfaction to some people to have a fling at Warrington. At a meeting not long since it was painted blacker than sin and the people who did that came from a town worse than Warrington. As to the smoke nu'sance, they had been deluged with printed matter about fuel economy and smoke ernission,and the firm with which he was connected hal spent hundreds of pounds in following some of these will-o-Ure-vvisps, withoutany SIICCf*S. The first substantial recommend- ation they I ad had arising from the discussion on the medical officer's report was ths idea of having a coroner for Warrington. That was a subject that the Council should take up very strongly. lie would like to call attention to insanitary buildings that were going up, not in Warrington but in the immediate suburbs. People went into houses Lefore they were ready to be occupied. They were both of old materials and were not dry before pin them. The Corporation was entitled re sleepingedwteomakerepresentations to the Local Govern- ment Board calling special attention to this evil that was menacing Warrington. It was ne easy thing foaa medical officer to go about condemning property here and there in Warrington. Some credit should be given to him for reduc:ng slum property as quickly as possible, but what good were hes efforts if they were not going to he supported by preventing the mischief that was going on outside the barough ? Councillor Wlrmsos said they did not want to see no smoke, because that would mean no work. Their object was to minimise the smoke nuisance, and for the ordinary raising of steam the mechanical stoksra could be used. If anyone wished to see an example of what could be done, they had ono in the Electricity Works where the sav:ng in fuel consumption in the first twelve months paid for the new appliances. Alderman BOLTON said that when a multitude of experts began to talk about what they did not under- stand and to give the medical officer advice as to how to do hie own business, he thought it was time the Council ceased to twaddle and let those who under- stood the business carry it out. (Hear, hear and laughter). Alderman BURTON maintained that theleading manrat- facturers of Warrington knew their busincas as well as anyone in the country, and if they could abate the smoke nuisance they would do so. The Health Com- inittee, he believed, were doing all they possibly could in various ways to minimise the smoke nuisance and to improve the health of the town. Alderman SMETHURST also referred to the suceess of the efforts put forth at the Electricity Works to economise fuel and abate the smoke nu!s ince. Ile believed Wilderspool Brewery had now adopted success- . ful methods for the same purpcse and that machinery was being put down at Whitecross to deal with the boilers which caused the greates smoke-output. Councillor AV. B. Roman's said that mechanical stokers had been adopted at Wilderspool and, in addition to doing away with the smoke : from the particular part of the works dealt with, they were now ' burning slack instead of coal. Councillor PEMBERTON said that could not apply to every trade. The great evil in Warrington was not smoke, nor even the size of the dwellings, but to a great extent it was the habits of the people. Did any- one wonder atrthe high infantile mortality, when they saw bow zone mot herd treated themselves and their children. A child nine months old had been fed on haddock. As to the demolition of insanitary property, if new houses were built at higher rents it would mean that two families would have to live in one dwelling. The real question to consider was the wages question. As to Councillor Fulton-Smith's remarks, the bye-laws of the Runcorn Rural District Council were even more stringent than those of the Warrington Corporation. Sufficient credit was not given to the Paving and Sewerage Committee for the progress that was being made in the paving of streets. They were proceeding with that work at such a rate that the pro- perty owners were crying out, and he had great sympathy with many of them. At the same time, the streets could not be left unpaved. The private street works undertaken during the past few years had cost £95,000 and at the present time they had the sanction of the Local Gove:nment Board for .£24,000 worth. He believed no house ought to be built until the street was channelled and paved, but at present there was no law to that effect. Alderman JAMES Evaers said that with regard to the smoke nuisance, on a more recent visitation it was found that no offender, save one, was exceeding the time allowed by half a minute. The inspectors were doing the best they could, and he (Alderman Evans) as chairman of the Health Committee, bad thought it his duty not to be in a hurry about menacing manufacturers who had enough to deal with, without being hampered and humbugged in the Police Court. The housing problem WLS a difficult one. The Council had before it now a list of eleven houses which it was sought to close. Councillor Pemberton had touched the crux of the question. If the small

POULTRY FOR EXHIBITION.

(Conanue(l,) FAKING.

Last week in the latter part of my artiele I just touched upon the practice of " Faking", exhibition specimens. Sometimes a novice is ignorantly guilty of so doing and to avoid same I propose giving a few details on this subject. It will be better first of all to understand what" Faking" in show. birds really means. To add to, or to take from, practically implies the real meaning, • So do not 'misunderstand if, when certain methods of improving the appear- ance of a bird are recommended that you would be guilty of" Faking," for such is not the case as we snail see as I proceed. Suppose you have a Minorca cockerel with a well serrated comb but it does not stand erect (generally so when the base of the comb is weak). In such a case it would be quite legitimate to use a comb-guard (a wire structure made to hold the .comb erect) to improve or rather, I should say that it would be necessary to do so if your bird was to stand a chance" of winning at all. But this could not be .called an improper practice. On the other hand suppose your bird had a comb which did stand erect but was deficient in serrations then, to artificially make one by cutting would be wrong, and would amount to "Faking." 'Again, removing "side sprigs " is against the rules. These are small side growths on the -comb and are -detrimental to the appearance of the bird. - Dyeing the feathers, colouring the legs, drawing superfluous feathers, trimming, all come under the heading of "faking" and I recommend my readers to refrain from such practices. If you do not happen to breed a few good ones the first season try again, and las your knowledge grows with experi- ence the honiours will duly come your _way. But whatever you do show them straight. You may ask then what to do with birds that are unfit for show purposes save only "faking ?" It does not always mean' that such birds are absolutely unfit for showing. Many a bird has won a prize with 'comb over' or even one With bad 'serrations in the comb. When it is a qUestion of doubt with you, and it almost seems not worth the entrance fee and your stock is not a large one. then I should not advise you to exhibit so doubtful a bird. Keep it in reserve for stock purposes. It will be opportune at this juncture to remind our readers that the dubbing of game fowls is recognised by the Poultry Club, and does not there- fore come under the heading of " Faking." Opinion on the

DUBBING

of Game fowls is divided and I suppose will remain so; but at the same tune while I-admit that it causes temporary pain, it certainly -adds to the appearance of the Game fowl as an exhibition bird. To those who, might have selected this variety as their choice a little advice on dubbing will be useful. I would not recommend you to experiment on a good specimen. You could practice operations on any fowl not -intended for either stock or show purposes until you .become efficient. The comb and wattles are best taken of wheri the cockerels are about 4 months old and this should be done with a proper pair of dubbing -scissors made and sold Specially for the purpose. The comb should first be removed with one clear cut from the back to front and after bathing in warm water place-. the bird in a warm pen away from the others until- the cut is healed. About a month after you could then remove the wattles in the same *ay but be careful not to cut too deep. Feed the birds well while thus penned up for they need good nourishing food to make up the loss of stamina caused through bleeding, If at all possible you should, before experimenting see a bird dubbed. This would be worth your while and very naturally assist you in acquiring correct methods. In fact it should be enforced that no one be allowed to dub ' a bird without such experience because it would ininimise pain if done after the manner adopted by experienced men. Some people are very much prejudiced against the dubbing of fowls. and I have already admitted that it causes pain to a certain extent, I can at the same time say something in its favour. In very severe and frosty weather it is against the comfort of fowls to have their combs frost-bitten,. and this frequently happens •in the North of England, and many a bird has been the victim of dubbing as a consequence. lam not pleading that all fowls should be dubbed because of this, but only to show that while the operation is a painful one, it often adds to the general comfort of the fowls afterwards. I have now said sufficient to show you what is, and what is not, ".Fling." There are other tricks of the ' Fancy which I think ought to be explained outside the question of actually tampering with the bird. The (1 • e, most serious one I think is that of marking the show pens in some way. If I relate a certain incident it would best explain my object. When visiting a large show in the North of England a few years ago, I was privileged by being admitted into the show while the birds Were being penned before judging. When the penning was completed I casually. observed each separate pen,and I noticed that the floor of each pen was covered with' short straw litter as is generally if not always the case. But on going round I also noticed that several pens had a good handful of hay as well as the litter supplied: by the authorities. I made a special note of the "pen number and on my second visit after judging was over I was not surprised to find that the occupants of the market pens had shared well in the prize list. It is needless for me to explain further. Sometimes a handful of oyster-shell grit is scattered in the pen, and other methods are adopted to make a distinction. This kind of thing is very detrimental to the growth' of the Fancy and while it is not for me to lecture in general, I -would strongly advise my readers to- boyeot such an exhibitor, and also make a special note of the judge when such instance occur. The absolute unfair- ness comes in when fanciers send their birds many miles to compete, while other more fortunate ones can travel with them and pen them at the show. A rather fierce controversy in the poultry press some years ago has done a lot to reduce such mean practices, and.l am hopeful of a better state of things in - the future. Fanciers iii general have need to thank the Press for a wide liberty in this direction, for it has done much to purify the tone.of the poultry fancy. And now a word on how to understand the different

===== 0002601_19090612_art0012.txt | included=true | included =====
COLOURS AND TYPES

of exhibition birds. It is impossible for you to keep very many varieties. At the same time you would like to understand what other varieties than your own are like. Theret,re I would advise you to obtain a coloured plate of each variety, or at least one each of the principal exhibition breeds., I would be glad to give information to any of my readers how to obtain tnese. You would always find such plates handy for reference and also for comparing notes when reading the reports of shows. All such details help you in fixing ideas and widening your knowledge in general on the subject of exhibition poultry. I feel that I must now conclude the subject on exhibition and give place to some remarks on utility, as this season of the year demands the attention of the "market man." Ther- efore I shall write on the " Goose " next week for, white it must be very exhilarating to lift a " cup " at the Dairy Show or the Palace, to carve the leg of a goose is often a fascinating occupation.

CUCKOO-SPIT.

This, according to "Gardening Illustrated," is formed by the larym of the common froghoppar (Aprophora spumaria). When in large numbers on the plant they cause a great deal of damage, as the amount i of sap they extract s very considerable, for they not only draw off enough to feed them.selves. but also to supply the froth, which must be continually evaporat- ing. The easiest way to destroy them is to carefully go dyer the plants, take them off with n small,4iflish brush, like a shaving-brush, washing this every new and hen in a little water.

NOTES ON GAMES, ETC.

Cricket.

Saturday was a poor day for cricketers, and the heavy rain caused the abandonment of a number of local matches. Warrington were able to play their League match with St. Helens at the glass and pill' centre, although in the early part of the afternoon the prospects of play were remote. However, there was an Improvement towards five o'clock and it was decided to go on with the match. With such a short time at the disposal of the players, the chances of a definite result were practically nil, and although Warrington made a brave and even brilliant attempt to win, they had to be content with a very creditable draw.

St. Helens had first turn at the wicket and their innings, which occupied an hour and forty minutes, realized 67. There was nothing particularly striking about their performance, the bowlers having the advantage, and the top score was that of the Rev. J. H. Watt who carried out his bat with 16 to his credit. Warrington had an hour in which to record a victory, aud they made the best use of their time, but with all their strenuous efforts they_could not get ahead, and when 7.30 arrived the total was 61 for seven wickets. The Warringtonians are deserving of congratulations on their plucky fight. and it was disappointing to finish with only six in arrears. T. Wright was in fine form and by hard hitting knocked up a very acceptable 25.

The Warrington bowling was chiefly shared by Cresswell and H. Callon who. captured 4 wickets for 19 runs and 5 wickets for 36 runs respectively:

The other League matches which had been arranged between Newton and Prescot first and second elevens were abandoned through rain.

The following are the League tables up-to-date : FIRST DIVISION.

In the Warrington and District Leagte St. James's, the champions, registered a remarkably easy victory at the expense ot Latchford Baptists. Dismissing their opponents for 4) , the Saints succeeded in pass- ing that total with not a single wicket down, C. bolt (29 not out) givinga splendid batting display. -

Cheshire Lines Engineers had also little difficulty in triumphing over Whitecross, the majority machine, the substantial figure of 52. J. Duckworth wielded the willow in splendid style and placed 41 to his credit.

• Walker's Brewery accounted for the "Guardian" Office by a margin of 4, with 2 wickets to spare. Low scoring was the rule, 11. Ditchfield, who made 20 for the printers, being the most successful batsman. L. Turner compilel a capital 13 not out for the victors.

In the Warrington and District Junior League there was an exciting contest between Orford and Itylands 2nd, the villagers running out winners by only two runs. ' Jolley captured 6 wickets for 10 runs and Williams 6 wickets for 11 runs. ,

Holy Trinity met more than their match in Sankey, who had the satisfaction of winning by 11 runs. The batting generally was "very tame, 11. Daintith (13) being the only player to reach double figures.

Appleton and Stretton accounted for Howley by a wicket and one run, and the batsmen in this match also had thb worst of matters, not a single player attaining double numbers.

A friendly match between St. Paul's choir and the Gospel Hall resulted in a win for the former by 26 runs, W. Hare batting very creditably for his 26.

Latchford Baptists trounced Marbury who only put up 30 against the Baptists' 95. J. Marshall (28 not out), T. Dixon (21), W. J. Hayes (17), and A. Albinson (13) gave fine batting displays.

The match between the Warrington Adult School and P. and K. was very keenly contested, the school eleven being victorious by 5 runs. It was rather curious that on the winning side the highest figure was "Extras 10." Rogers (16) and Johnson (15) did good work for P. and K.

Bowling.

Local bowlers will be gratified at the success of Lancashire and Cheshire, the holders of the British Crown Green Association • Championship, who won their first county match of the season at Flixton on Saturday, beating, Staffordshire by -82 points. The Lancashire and Cheshire captain, J. W. G. Coombs, retained his unbeaten record, scoring over the Stafford- shire captain by 6 points. Three Warrington players —A. Crleave, T. Ellison and A. Scott—won rather easily Although credited with victory the Lancashire and Cheshire Association had last season's majority reduced from 145 to 82.

Monday saw the close of the 20th individual merit competition in connection with the L. and C. Associa- tion. We are sorry that the honour has --riot come to Warrington this year, but J. Weave, of . Warrington, made a plucky fight and was only knocked out in the semi-final. The green at Chorlton was in good condi- tion and a fair number of spectators were present when , the final stages Were reached. In the semi- finals Arnold and Brooks played a much better game than did Glover and Gleave. Atnold, especially on the short length, was a treat to watch, and Brooks had to keep on playing through. ' He had hard lines on two or three occasions, but the Didsbury representa- tive fully deserved the honour of competing in the final. J. Weave went well away from Glover, and -once led 13-7. Then Glover had a run of 12, and eventually Won 21-14. The final therefore lay between T. Glover and 11. Arnold, and, an interesting contest it proved. At the 18th end they were 'l2 all: It was at this point that Glover showed his best form. The Leigh lad bowled some splendid woods on a most peculiar mark. and went to 18-12 and secured the L. and 0. Championship for the second time by 12-13. This is the fifth time he has appeared in the final, and, he has also won the Manchester and District Individual Merit. . .

The most keenly contested game in the match of the Works Bowling* Competition on, Saturday was that bet Ween llylands No. 1 (sc.) and Penketh Tannery (10), the former only winning by 5 points.

The contest between J. Lloyd (Penketb) and R. Savory (Itylands) was a very interesting one, the players being very evenly matched. Their figures mounted steadily up, and they peeled at 10. - Savory got another 3, but before he could score any more his opponent raised his figures to 14. However, Salrory equalled his figures but was not successful, for his opponent with the next wood secured the required number. Another good match was provided by H. Bowyer (Penketh) and A. Stringer (Rylands). The Penketh trundler's figures stood at 11 to Stringer's six, but Stringer by a fine effort peeled with him at 11. Bowyer got two more, and a moment or two later -his opponent drew level with him. However, the Penketh man reached the top score, to the delight of his supporters. .

That the match between Castle Rubber (30) and Armitage and Rigby (10) was strikingly evident. The contest resulted in a win for the former by 58 points. It is only fair to state that W. Tavlin for Armitage and Rigby had a walk-over, but for the most part the play of the Castle Rubber was but poor. For the losers the Captain F. O'Brien displayed considerable ability. His opponent W. Rostern was level with him at 9, but he eventually won by a majority of 3.

Rielnitonds had an invaluable advantage over Orford

Tannery No. I, at the commencement by 55 points, which consisted of three walk-overs and handicap of 20 to Orford's handicap of 10. J. Cooper (Richmond's) and J. Andrews (Orford) provided some interesting play. Cooper led by 15-6 and they peeled out at 11 and again at 13. Cooper scored two more points and thus triumphed.

Another interesting game in the same match was that between B. Naylor (Richmonds). and J. Povey (Orford). Naylor gained 6 to his opponents 1, but Povey steadily pulled up reaching 5 before Naylor could register any more. However, Richmond's representative went on finely, making his total 11 to his contestant 5. Povey got another 2, and then Naylor added 1. In a few minutes they peeled at 12, and shortly afterwards the figures announced were Povey 15, Naylor 14.

A friendly match was played at Runcorn on Saturday between Runcorn B.C. and the Warrington Junior Conservatives. All the Runcorn players had top scores, winning by 180 to 122. '

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Th 3 New Building Scheme. TENDER ACCEPTED.

The fortnightly meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday, the Rev. T. R. Matthews presiding, There were also present :—Miss Broadbent., Al's. Simpson, Mrs. Coiling- wood, Rev. M. Ryan, Dr. Nfurray, Messrs. G. Mather, A. J. Jolle,v E. L. Bent, 3. Billinge. W. Gill, R. Bolton, R. Lee, W. Form, W. C. Brooks, P. Longton, W. Houghton, J. Cook, M. W. Seton, A., Brooks, R. Forshs.w, A. Bottomley (clerk), R. W.. Peacock (assistant clerk), J. F. Awbery (master) and A. Wright (superin- tendent of the Cottage Homes).

MR. G A TiNETT'S RESIGNATION. ALTERATION OF THE DISTRICTS.

Ata meeting of the Finance Committee a letter VMS read from Mr. T. .7. Garnett resigning his appointment as relieving officer of the No. 1 District and asking the Guardians when fixing his superannuation allowance to kindly take into consideration the extra work he has had to perform during the past two years both in regard to relief duties and the work put upon him by the Old Age Pension Act, with a view of adding a few years to his present completed service viz ,20 years. It was recommended that the Guardians place on record their high appreciation of the valued and faithful ser- vices rendered by Mr. Garnett as an officer of this Union extending over a period of 29A years. With regard to the superannuation allowance to be paid to Mr. Garnett, the committee consider that his applica- tion for a few years to be added to his present service is deserving of some consideration and they unani- mously recommend that a period of four years be added to his present service for the purpose of computing his superannuation allowance namely 33-COtla's of £lB2, making a yearly sum of £lOO 2e., to be due to him. With regard to the appointment of a successor,the com- mittee, having in mind the large number of persens residing in the present No.l Relief District who are in 'receipt of relief, and the large amount of work to be per- formed by the relieving officer, are of opinion that the duties cannot be satisfactorily performed by one officer and they recommend that thetnion be divided into four relief districts as follows :—No. 1 District to comprise Warrington Township ( ['own Hall Ward, St. John's Ward and Orford Ward) ; No. 2 District to comprise Haydock. Newton and Burtonwooa Townships ; No. 3 District to 'comprise Houghton, Middleton and Arbury, Poulton-with-Fearnh .ad, Rixton-with- Glezeibrook, Soutftworth-with-Crofr, Winwick-aith- Martinscroft, War, ington (Fairfield, Latchford and Howley Wards) ; No. 4 District to comprise Cuerdley, are tt Sankey, and Penketh' Warrington (St. Austin's, Whitecross and Bewsey Warcls). By this pro- posed alteration the boundaries of the relief districts will be co-terminous with the municipal ward boundaries within the county barou:h and, as near as.possible, the population in each relief district will be equalised. The Committee recommend that the office used by Mr. Garnett at the Workhouse be used in future as the relief office for the proposed No. 4 district and that a room in the clerk's offi .o at Bewsey Chambers be us.el as a relief office for the proposed No. 1 district; th it the relief pay station in Cairo-street be dispensed with and that the relief to persons resident in the No. 1 and 4 districts be paid by the relieving officer at their homes. The Committee recommend that in future a s., stem of cross-visiting the persons in receipt of relief be inaugurated under the direction of the Clerk ; also that advertisements be inserted in the local papers inviting applications for the appointment of two relieving officers, each at a salary of .C1(0 per annnm, rising £lO per annum, provided the duties are performed satisfactorily, to a maximum of £l3O per annum.. A return prepared by the Clerk showed that the No. 1 District of which Mr. Garnett has had sole charge has a population of 48,600, the amount of relief paid weekly being £52 4s. Sti. The re-arrangement will create anew dibtriat and reduce the No. 1 district to an area with a population of 25,200, with weekly relief to the amount of £35 10s. Mr. JOLLEY in moving the adoption of the minutes 7ald a tribute to Mr. Garnett's-Ltitnia services. He said Mr. Garnett htd had to look after foar-ninths of the p.rpulation while the rem Lining five-ninths had had two officers allotted to it. . Mr. HOUGHTON seconded and said Mr. Garnett was a consdetitious and efficient servant.

lue minutes were apptoved and the proposals as to Mr. Garnett's superannuation will he brought forward in the form of, a resolution at the meeting on July 21st.

THE NEW BUILD LNG.-TENDER ACCEPTED. Six tenders for the erection of a now building for the accommodation of aged mid infirm inmates were submitted, the respective amounts being (I) £5,157 ; (2) £5;221 ; (3) £5,077 ; (4) .25,150 ; (5) £5,116 195.. ; (6) £5,245.—N0 names were divulged before the choice was made, and the Board decided provisionally to accept Noe lowest tender—that for £5,077. which it was after- wards stated had been sent in by Mr. Harry Fairclough. he CLERK said the tender included the cost of the building and also of yard walls an! unclimbable fences. The-nther items—yards and roadways—were over and above the contract and would involve extra expenditure. The architect's estimate for the contract was higher than any of the tenders which had been submitted, so that the Board was on the safe side, as far as the amount was concerned. When the Local Government Board's approval was received, fourtc en days notice would be given to every Guardian before the final decision as to proceeding with the work VMS arrived at. MRS. WATER POLO. WARRINGTON V. WIDNES.

The seccdati.in-ritc-h of the water polo seasonwas played at the Legh-street Baths on Tuesday evening, when Warrington met Widne-. There was a good attend Ince. R. Peers, an old Warrington player,helpei Widnes, and was responsible for three gca's scored by them. In the first half Widnes were called upon to defend the deep end, which they did with vigour. Peers opened the scoring for Widnes, which was followed by one from It: Pepper for Warrington shortly afterwards. The visitors were then seen in good form, and the second goal was scored, S. Ince eventually equalising. The interval arrived with two goals each.—On resuming an Attack was made upon the Warrington goal, a stinging shot being smartly saved by the home custodian. Later, however, R. Peers evaded with his clever tactics, arid added to the Chemical town's score. More smart play by Warrington was witnessed, which culminated in Pepper again scoring. Time arrived with the result : Warrington, three goals ; Widnes three coals. ( A squadron race between Widnes and Warrington took place. The Widnesians' powerful swimming pro Ned too much for Warrington, who, hoirever, stuck to their task manfully, with the result that Widnes only won by three yards. Mtp.h interest was taken in a 50 yards members: handicap. There were 20 entries, and the race was swum in four heats. The final wieners were :-1 W. Jolley ; 2, H. Silk; 3, W. Dunn.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, JUNE 12TH, 1909.

The division on the THE second reading of the CHANCELLOR'S Finance Bill was taken , TRIUMPH. on Thursday night, the Government securing the handsome majority of 157. Among -Liberals there was no apprehension of the Tesult, though the Irish members were said -to be returning to the House to vote in a body 'a,gainst the Bill. Their attitute to the "oudget is that of the ballad of Macpherson, Which, as will be remembered,concludes With 'the striking and emphatic lines: • " This is all my tale, Sirs, I hope 'tis new t'ye ! Here's your fery good healths, And hang ta whuskey duty !" 'rile Irishmen have no special grievance in this case,and it seems a. pity that they should lrY to make one. But they do not now hold -the balance in the House of Commons, and they may therefore feel perfectly free to' Inake demonstrations. The Government has 112 t only a large majority independently of -‘lle Irish representatives,b ut it has produced P°Pular Budget. Violently hostile speeches in the House produce no corresponding excite- lent outside. The truth appears to be that LLOYD-GEORGE'S financial proposals :tave inspired a new hope in the people. Here, lhey seem to have said, is a Budget that con- something new and encouraging and t the same time just and practical. There s 110 real opposition to the Budget out of °°rs, where, indeed, it is regarded as a I'atim-r-r glorious experiment. Reflections not unlike this may have been 13assing through the mind of the CHANCELLOR 'rim EXCHEQUER on Wednesday, when e //lade his brilliant reply to his crities. It IR the fashion among Tory journalists and eakers to pretend that Mr. LLOYD GEORGE not serious because he is scintillating. II "se to whom he replied on Wednesday will IliardlY be of that opinion at the present s')lllent. The CHANCELLOR OF THE „ti XCREQUER was as lively as usual, but with v3eacilY. effect. There is a popular saying out' wiping the floor with one's oppon- ,:.rit's." That is what Mr. LLOYD GEORGE ,ciiril‘'• He was preceded by Mr. BONAR LAW, 11() is believed to entertain expectation of day being CHANCELLOR OF THE CHEQUER himself. This gentleman appeairs those who are not well-informed to cram sPeeches with facts. Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE Nall well within the truth when he said Mr. •Th'cAlt LAW'S statements are not facts at all. sPeech, said the CHANCELLOR OF THE pit. CI-INVER, had one feature in common -be'lt.b all the other speeches of the same mem- :"-that its " facts " were utterly unreliable. 11.1 Characteristic of Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE tql When he says anything of this kind, he the°,,'les proceeds to prove it. The author Of tolifu'udget is not fighting a real case. He is itlw,l,ted by guesses and suppositions and itll.d°/15. His own case is clearly made out Ntasuls method of opposition. If the Budget .it 2 bad and a wicked one, as is pretended, W°lllcl not only be possible, but easily

possible, to face it with valid arguments and reasonable alternatives. No alternatives have been produced, so far, at least, and of the arguments employed, the less said the better.

One contention that the CHANCELLOR OF THE EXCHEQUER took much pleasure in routing was that put forward by Mr. HAROLD Cox and others, to the effect that the present Budget is in reality a Budget of revenge. As he showed with easy triumph, this is a view of the facts that could only be sustained if there were no wealthy Liberals, or if there were no Liberals who drink beer and whisky But what about Scotland? asked the CHAN- CELLOR. It is deeply and almost formidably interested in the distillery business, and yet it is almost solid in its support of the Govern- ment. The idea that anything like revenge is contemplated is refuted by the circumstances. The Government, if it entertained any such design as that, would not only strike its opponents butt its friends. However, Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE placed his defence on higher grounds. He said that any Government against which it would be possible to sustain the charge of penalising its political oppon- ents would be unfit for office, and ought to be impeached. That is true, no doubt. But we have had examples of such Governments, and one of them not very long ago Went to wreck and ruin at the polls. As to the present Administration, no charge could possibly be much more foolish than that which Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE so energetically and conclusively rebutted, and as to some of the main proposals of his Budget he was able to show that, in days comparatively recent, they have received the joyful support of leading members of the Opposition.

The Lymm Women's LYMM WOMEN Liberal Association has LIBERALS. started its career under the most encouraging circumstances and we have no doubt that it is destined to play an important and useful part in political work in the division. A happier inaugural gathering than the garden party held in the beautiful grounds of Lymm Hall on Wednes- day afternoon could not have been desired, and the enthusiastic 'optimism which pervaded the proceedings was full of promise for the future. Mr. and Mrs. F. H. Smith have the interests of Liberalism truly at heart, and the cordial thanks of the associa- tion are due and were freely accorded to them for their kindness in endeavouring to further the cause by inviting the members to such a pleasant function amidst really delightful surroundings. The presence of Mrs. W. J. Crossley; the esteemed wife of the popnlar and hard-working member for the division, afforded the liveliest satisfaction to the assembly, and the charming little address in which she dealt with current topics in a simple yet very convincing way, was highly appreciated and will be long and pleasurably remembered.

The general public will A BOON FOR THE be interested in the TRAVELLER, announcement that the Lancashire and York- shire, the London and North-Western, and the Midland Railway Companies- have agreed, commencing forthwith, that ordinary and tourist tickets, with some few exceptions, will be available in the trains, of any of the three companies, affording a direct service between stations for which the tickets are available. The whole or any portion of the journey may be made on any of ,the three companies' lines. These are facilities which railway travellers will greatly appreciate. One of the disadvantages of our present rail- way systems is the want of intercommunica- tion such as would be imperative on State rail- ways. The various companies Will be wise in extending facilities such as those detailed above.‘ We are tempted by the innovation to ask whether the Cheshire Lines and the L. and N.-W. cannot come to some similar arrangement by which travellers between Warrington, Manchester and Liverpool might use either service with the same ticket. This would be a boon very greatly appreciated in the districts affected and particularly in Warrington.

Warrington Walking THE ORIGIN OF Day is securely fixed in WALKING DAY. the town's calendar and

seems likely to remain its most popular institution. No one, how- ever,seems very clear as to its origin. It was held for many years on the same day as Newton Races, and a popular opinion is that it was so placed because the promotors desired it to serve as a counter attraction to 'the races. The only record we have been able to find which may throw any light upon the inStitution of the scholars' day is one which tells that the Hon. Horace Powys, Rector from 1832 to 1854, planned to celebrate the anniversary of the Church Sunday school in an imposing way. When he became Rector there was only one school, but he was able, by the help of subscriptions he collected, to build another in Church-street. The anni- versary as arranged by him consisted in a field day for the children, on which, after attending church and hearing a sermon, they spent the afternoon and were entertained to milk and buns in St. Elphin's field. This was in the early thirties.' It seems quite possible that the Nonconformists followed his example and gave a treat to their Sunday school scholars on the same clay. We give this, however, ats a guess, and shall be glad if any reader has better knowledge.

The Health of the THE HEALTH OF Borough is one of those THE BOROUGH. disquietingtopics which

thrust themselves prominently before us every year, lead to considerable discussion, and advance very slowly towards the stage when they may be considered complacently and with some satis- faction. Perhaps it would be a good

thing if medical officers' reports were quarterly instead of annual diagnoses of public ill-health. Then we might get a closer and more persistent . application to remedial measures by the responsible authorities. The high death-rate, the terrible infantile mortality, the slums a d the smoke are deplored fervently every year. but instead of being minimised they grow blacker and more threatening. Warring- ton leads the Way among many manu- facturing towns for the facility with which it kills off its inhabitants. The distinction is certainly not one to be envied, and we feel compelled once again to urge upon the Health Committee the need for strenuous effort in combating the numel•ous evils which impair the vitality of the town- dweller. Tuesday's di scussion,though it pro- ceeded largely on familiar lines,was far from unprofitable. It gave some indication of the Heath Committee's renewed determination to press forward with reforms, and it was illuminated by at least one valuable sug- gestion. Councillor Dr. Joseph, as the sole medical man on the Council, carries weight whenever he speaks on questions affect- ing the public liealth, and his view that the time had come when Warrington should have a town's coroner, received con- siderable support and no adverse criticism. We believe he is right in arguing that the fear of an inquest on the least suspicion as to the causes of death will result in much greater care being taken of the infant life in the town. There are limits to what an indefatigable Coroner like Mr. Brighouse can accomplish in the wide area of South-West Lancashire, and it is no reflection upon him to say that a Coroner for Warrington might have considerable influence on that 4 per cent. of uncertified deaths which causes such dis- quietude. This is only one remedy for one evil, but it is important enough to demand serious attention. We hope Dr. Joseph will pursue his suggestion to its logical and satis- factory sequel.

The most striking

A THE LORD feature of Monday ADVOCATE AND THE evening's debate on the 'LAND TAXES. Finance Bill was the Lord Advocate's brilliant and admirably reasoned defence of the land taxes, upon which the artillery of the Opposition has been especially concen- trated. He met the whole case of the adver- sary point by point, and demolished it. But there were two far-reaching, deep-rooted principles elaborated in his speech which deserve special consideration. and which brought the debate back fi orn trivial or technical disputes to the solid foundation which is reached when political questions are illuminated by the light of perennial human interests, instead of being obscured by the dialectic of the partisan. Is national financ to be made a juggle amongst powerful inter- ests, each eager to throw off the burden of national obligations from its own shoulders, or shall we, as a nation, recognise the essential justice of the principle that obliga- tions should be inseparable from privileges? It is often made an accusation against democratic policies that they set class against class. But if any one is guilty of this crime, it is he who tries to persuade the classes which derive the greatest benefit by the security afforded by our natural defences and the creation of a healthy environment and an efficient supply of labour through social legislation, that they should exact the cost of these national privileges from those who can least afford to pay for them. And if we accept the principle that property should bear its fair share of the expenditure which enhances its value, gives to it security, and makes its possession enjoyable, then we shall have little difficulty in agreeing that the first call upon property should come where its value has been largely the creation of the community. And that is just what the land taxes do.

Mr. J. J. Webster, who has been appointed consulting engineer for the Warrington Bridge Scheme, has promised to send in his designs in the course of a few days and he will probably attend to explain them to the Bridges Sub-Committee next week. * * *

Our congratulation; to Alderman James Evans, who received the unanimous support of the Town Council on Tuesday when he succeeded to the Aldermanic vacancy caused by the death of the late Alderman Ellison. There had been rumours of party, bickering for the vacant seat during the week, but fortunately there was no attempt at the meet- ing to question Mr.Evans' right to succession as the senior Councillor.

* * * The Water Committee having at last raised a profit from a: profitless past,is about to embark on further large expenditure. Councillor Pemberton uttered serious words of warning about the tendency to spend which seemed to be once more permeating the Council. His references to the Lawsen Contract were no more palatable than his prophecy that the new scheme would cost £lOO,OOO. We hope his prophetic vision will not prove to be as accurate in this case as it was in the last, * * *

Foreign trade is wearing a more smiling aspect. The returns which the Board of Trade publish on Tuesday show increases in imports and re-exports,and,although exports are down by nearly 5 per cent, in comparison with May of last year, the decline is mainly due to lower prices, and to the fact that the month had a working day less. As for the increase in imports, we regard it with great cheerfulness, for the really important increases are in raw materials, and the imported raw material of May is the exported manufacture of June and July. Cotton and wool in particular are coming in well,so that improvement may be looked for in depressed Lancashire. * * *

Mrs. A. H. Crosfield is rapidly securing for herself a warm place in the hearts of War- rington people by her enthusiasm for her husband's cause, her deep interest in social and religious work locally, and last but by no means least, her personal charm. She had quite a busy day on Thursday, when, the Member being engaged in London, she deputised for him at the opening of the Ellesmere-street Mission sale of work, and delivered a very bright little speech, paid'a flying visit to the garden fête at Latchford, and hurried back to London early the same evening. She is so well-qualified for undertaking useful public work that we hope she may long have the pleasure of being the wife of the Member for Warrington.

The Borough Member and his wife gave a dinner on Tuesday night at which they entertained Mr. McKenna (First Lord of the Admiralty) and Mrs. McKenna, Mr. John Burns (President of the Local Government Board), Mr. Alfred Emmott (Chairman of Committees in the House) and Mrs. Emmott, Mr. Tomkinson, M.P. for the Crewe Division, and Miss Tomkinson ; Mr. C. Nicholson,M.P.,

and Mrs. Nicholson (the latter of whom will be remembered locally as the late Mr. George Crosfield's daughter), Mr. and Mrs. Alfred Mond, M.P., Miss Crosfield, Mips Greenwood, and Colonel Henry A. Fletcher.

* 0 Dr. Clifford, writing on the Secular Solution, says: I find four classes of advocates of 'secular' education :—(1.) Those who would not allow any use what- ever of any part of the Bible in State schools. These are very few, and I have never heard of a teacher amongst them. (2) Those who would simply read the Bible. (3) Those who would read the Bible, and give what Dr. Angus called ' lexical ' explanations—i.e., such as would be given by a teacher in the use of Milton or Bunyan. (4)- Those—and they form the vast majority—who stand for the use of portions of the Bible suited to the capacity of the children, such use to be literary, historical, ethical, and spiritual; but never in any way theological or ecclesiastical. Sure as we are men .that last is the just way and the way the nation has to go." - * * * At the Annual Meeting of the Church Defence Committee, the Archbishop of Canterbury pleaded for absolute courtesy and that spirit of reasonable charity which refuses to impute to opponents unworthy motives of any kind. The Rev. Griffith Thomas, Organizing Secretary of the Church Defence Committee in South Wales, does not pay much heed to the Arthbishop's admoni- tion. Speaking at a recent meeting in Car- marthen he spoke as follows : " Disestablish the devil if you like, but it was a mark of the decadence of a nation when one beheld it clamouring for the disestablishment of God." If this gentleman's mental vision were not so distorted he might realise that there was a devil within himself which requires dis- endowment.

We regret (says" The Liberator ") that a newspaper usually so well-informed as the "Daily News" should have conveyed to its readers the impression that there was hardly any Nonconformity in Wales prior to the Methodist Revival. No doubt the revival gave a mighty impetus to Nonconformity, but the seed was widely sown long before that period. When charles II published his first Indulgence, licences for preaching were issued for more than a hundred places in the Principality, and in 1715, according to Rees' "History of Nonconformity," there were above 110 distinct congregations besides a considerable number of recorded dwelling- houses where religious services were occasion- ally held. Dr. Rees- calculates that at this time one-eighth of the population of Wales were Nonconformists. For nearly two hundred years since then the Welsh clergy have continued to enjoy the national endow- ments, but they, have lost the great majority of the people. notions

The important collection of pictures and water-colour drawings, which was formed by the late Mr. Holbrook Gaskell, o Woolton, has been catalogued for sale at Christie's towards the end of this month. Mr. Gaskell (says the London cbrrespondent of the "Liverpool Post and Mercury") with the unselfishness of the true connoiseur and real lover of pictures, afforded the public not only of Liverpool, but of other towns. many opportunities of viewing his artistic posses- sions. He was at different times a con- tributor to Exhibitions in Liverpool, Man- chester, Warrington, Blackburn,Birmingham, Glasgow, Newcastle, and other places, and in this way a knowledge has been gained of the high character of the collection which was housed at Woolton. But though the collection has long enjoyed the repu-' tation of being one of the finest of the modern English school in private owner- ship, it will only be when the whole of the pictures and drawings are displayed for inspection in the spacious galleries in King-street, London, that those outside the circle of Mr. Gaskell's intimate friends will be able to form a proper idea of their value and importance. Practically all the masters of the school are represented in the collection by important examples—Constable, David Cox, De Wint, Luke Fildes, Peter Graham, Holland Hook, J. L. Lewis, Linnell, Millais, J. Phillip Stanfield, Alma Tadema, Landseer, and Turner. There are more works by David Cox in the catalogue than have come under the hammer at a single sale for some time past.

MAL COM TI3AINIE.

" An Abandoned Woman."

A clever play of the sensational type, With the above 41eat1y descriptive title, has proved a popular attrac- tion at the Royal Court Theatre this week. It is the story of a fascinating but unscrupulous French woman, the "queen" of a gang of thieves, who is ever scheming to compass the downfall of those who are unfortunate enough to cross her path. Excitement is the dominant note, but there is a strain] of the pathetic, and interest is enhanced by some comic interludes. Mr. Harry Foxwell's company gives •a capable interpretation, Miss Hilda Beverley distinguish- ingherself by a remarkably realistic-study of "Ninon." She is convincing, without over-doing the . part, and she works upon the feelings of the gallery with such effect that she is heartily "booed " and hissed. Mr. Charles Vivian, as Phillip° Malvoisin, is a capital stage villain, and plays a conspicuous part in the development of the drama. Mr. Teddy Myles proves a general favourite as Paddy Boyle, an irrepressible son of Erin, who causes many, an outburst of merri- ment, and he is well seconded by Mr. Will Glaze as Sammy Sharp, - a street waif whose intelligence is highly developed. Mr. „Tom Beasley is a dashing. Captain Henry Seaton, and Miss Roma Pendrous infuses pathos into her portrayal of Eleanor Dean. The mounting of the play is all that could be desired ' and the orchestral selections, under Mr. Norman Latham's conductorship, are a pleasing feature.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Shiress Will, K.C., Messrs. John Mainwaring and Co., auctioneers, Bridge-street, Warrington, sued Samuel Taylor, cattle dealer, Wins- ford, for £2l 2s. 6d. balance of accOunt.—Mr. A. Bankes, assistant curate at St. Paul's Church, Warrington, who has special charge of the work at All Saints' Mission, was ordained priest at the service held in the Cathedral Church of St. Peter, Liverpool, on Sunday morning. He expects' to ba back in Warrington shortly. The Rev. L. B. Radford, whom some of our readers may remember as second master of the Grammai School several years ago, during which time he was Sunday curate in Trinity Parish, has, says Holy Trinity Parish Magazine, just gone to Australia to enter upon the post of Warden of St. Paul's College in connection with the University of Sydney. He sends a post card mentioning that on his way out he called at Albany, of which the Venerable Archdeacon Louch (curate of Trinity Church nearly 30 year ago) is the rector. By a fortunate chance he met the Arch- deacon near his church, to whom he was quite a stranger. Warrington P.S.A.. _ _ _ - -

The weekly meeting of the Warrington P.S.A Society was held at Friars Green House on Sunday. Mr. al uir presided and gave an address on" Faith, the substance of things not seen." Mr. A. Charnock, of Leigh, spoke upon "A good name is better than great riches." He contended that in the majority of cases, the presence of wealth was the forerunner of indolence, sloth, and often ended in degradation and death,while, on the other hand, men who had earned a good name were in nearly every case those who had passed through many obstacles, difficulties and privations, find thus step by step had fought their way upward in fluence of onward, never forgetaing the sustaining of the Great Creator. A vote of condolence was passed with the family of the late Mr. Makin in their sad bereavement.

Remarkable Engineering Feat. An interesting engineering feat was on Monday carried out on the Mersey, when Messrs. Cammell, Laird and Company, who are reconstructing part of the New Brighton Landing-stage, lifted the north bridge from its position and conveyed it by tugs up the river, a distance 'of about four miles, to their Tranmere Bay works. The bridge was placed on "camels " belonging to the Mersey Docks and Harbour Board, and successfully floated' to its destination. This calls to mind an even more remarkable feat of engineering and navigating skill which was per- formed thtrteen years ago. An addition of 400 feet was about to be added to the north-end of the Liver- pool Landing-stage. The iron framework was put together upon pontoons at the West Float, Birken- head. The contractors were Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Limited, and the work was begun in April, 1890, and completed a month later. The huge structure was then towed along the Birkenhead Docks and across the river, and so accurate were the calculations of the engineers that it fitted exactly into position, all that had to be done being tt rivet the existing portion of the stage and the new part together, and then put on the deck. In passing through the dock entrances the work of navigation by the tugs was so nicely =tinged that although there weie only 18 inches of playroom the paint on the ironwork was not even scratched.—` Liverpool Courier."

Rev. H. Dunnico on Religion and Politics. Preaching in Kensington Chapel, Liverpool, on Sunday evening, the Rev. Herbert Dunnico said that while it was the mission of the Christian Church to point the way to heaven, it must do 80 by way of • a better life on earth. When labour was speeded up to a poirit that a man was used up and laid upon the • shelf at forty years ord, when new inventions and: combinations of capital forced the cost of living up and the ckances of employment down, of what earthly use was it to tell a man of the glories beyond? What men most needed was the chance to live a .decent, upright lift,protect?on for children,and the right to work for the support of his family. How could they expect a man to live a consistent Christian life denied the opportunity of earning his daily bread ? If further proof of that were needed, it was to be found in the fact that the very century which had witnessed the development 'of competitive era had also witnessed the growth of an ever-widening gulf between the Church and the masses. A Church that did not take its part in solving this problem was unfaithful to Christ. They might pray till, like " James the Just," the callosities on their knees were like those of a camel, and the evils would still go on unless they claimed the sphere of politics for Christ, and by Act of Parliament put a stop to them. The spirituality of many critics was mere humbug and toadyism on the one hand and wicked cowardice on the' other.

GARLAND, Bridge st., Warrington.—Advt. - Warringtonian's Narrow Escape from a Bomb. Mr. Charles Gorse, second son of Mr. J. Gorse, Sankey Bridges, who has been in South America for several years, had a remarkably narrow escape front being blown to pieces at Buenos Ayres a few weeks ago. There have been serious riots and disturbances in the city for some timj- and many lives have beea lost in conflicts between the military and the populace. News has just been received from Mr. Gorse that he was recently riding in one of the city trams, and noticed near the conductor a large basket of vegetables —apparently a very harmless cargo. But on the car stopping Mr. Gorse luckily heard a ticking sound coming from the neighbourhood of the basket and informed the conductor. The latter at once lifted the basket on to the sidewalk, but before the car had moved away a bomb concealed among the vegetables exploded and killed seven people- outright. The car was partly wrecked, but Mr. Gorse had the good fortune to be quite uninjured. His many friends in Warrington will, we are sure, be glad to hear of his Providential escape. Death of Mrs. James Reiland. We regret to announce the death of Mrs. James Skelland, wife of Councillor James Skelland, 63, Bewsey-street, who passed away Last Sunday evening. The deceased lady, who was 70 years of age, had been ailing for some time, but death came unexpectedly through a sharp attack of pneumonia.Mrsßkelland took considerable interest in her husband's work in con- nection with the Ragged School and Mission,and made Many personal sacrifices to enable him to carry on his work there. She worshipped at Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church where she was greatly respected. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Wednesday in the presence of a large number of relatives and friends. --, - An Expensive Trench.

At the Warrington County Court on Thursday, before his Honour Judge Shiress Will, K.C.; William Boles, general dealer, 79, Wash-lane, Latchford, sued Leonard Unsworth„ 7, Norris-street, for :tlO 10s. damages for alleged trespass.—Mr. A. Browne appeared for the plaintiff and Mr. C. H Unsworth defended.— Plaintiff's case was that on April 29th, defendant, who was the incoming tenant of plaintiff's premises, illegally and wrongfully trespassed upon plaintiff's land and stables and dug a deep 'trench across the road or cartway, thus preventing plaintiff taking his horse and cart out of the stable In consequence plaintiff alleged that he had suffered considerable loss. For the defence it was contended that plaintiff was leaving Warrington and had sold his sheds, &c., to defendant under terms which implied immediate 'possession. Defendant went on the land with plaintiff's permission, and only dug the trench for gardening purposes. The claim for ten guineas, said Mr. Unsworth, was preposterous because the trench could have been filled up by the expenditure of half-a-crown. —Plaintiff said he distinctly stipulated that defendant should leave the roadway clear.—After a long hear- ing, his Honour gave judgment for the plaintiff for for £1 and costs. PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

The most popular turn at the Palace and Hippodrome this week may be said to be" Casey's Court. The court consists of .30 urchins and an old woman. These urchins provided very good fun. The stage presented a grotesque appearance. In the centre was the " Orkestra," in which several musicians were. playing with crude instruments. On the right were the stalls, which looked far from comfortable, and on the "left was the pit, where the seats offered were not at all enviable. A gentleman schoolboy appeared on the scene, and being- made aware that an entertain- ment was about to commence asked the price of The accommodation, and was answered by the cashier. " Froth two bottles to three halfpence.'' Thereupon he asked for a box and was immediately shown to a bath. This was too much for him, and he objected, but he was promptly thrown in much to his discomfiture. Most of the children sang in a way worthy of the support of the audience. The Great Albas is quite above the ordinary run of wire performers. His most daring feat is when he walks up to the circle on a wire and then slides down to the stage on his head. Ernesto seems to be able to juggle with seven balls quite easily, and can also juggle with three carving knives. His best feat is to place a lighted lamp on the end of a bar, balance this .on his forehead, reach two concertinas, play a popular air, and walk up a ladder and down again on the other side. M'dlle De Rose, who can play a piano with remarkable ability, and possesses a bass voice, is assisted by Miss Hilda Claremont. a girl soprano. They delight the audience with their singing to such an extent that they claim.repeatcd encores. Mark Milton, comedian sings some capital songs, and of course puts a joke in as he goes along. J. H. Hawtrey, sings of bis success in Anstralia, and the Bros. Connolly engage in very smart repartee. The cinematograph pictures are of_a very interesting character.

"PETER PAN "SUMMONED. F..alvation Army's Critic Fails to Appear.

For a week or more an individual calling himself "Peter P.sn " has inflicted himself on the town and created some disorder by the holding of open-air meetings at which he has mercilessly attacked the Salvation Army and its work. His state- ments have been wild and extravagant, but they have been couched in such a form as to gull the more susceptible of his hearers and cause a feeling of suspicion about the Salvation 'Army and its leaders which is wholly unjustified. The man has been going about the country carrying on this campaign, and apparently living on the proceeds of his collections. The police 11,1ve at last had to take the matter up. and at the Police Court yesterday a summons was heard charging "Peter Pan," of 69, 13uttermarket-street, with unlawfully -and wilfully obstructing the passage at Arpley on June 4th and sth. The defendant did not appear.

he Clerk : The police summons was served at his residence in Warringt on. The Chief Constable said it would be better if he Alertly reviewed the facts of what they knear of "Peter Pan" in Warrington, and as he did not appear that moraing he would ask for a warrant. The defendant bad •g,-,no to Liverpool. It wculd appear that the defendant was an idle and worthless loafer, who on Tuesday week last put in an appearance in Warrington and started gathering a small crowd at Bride-foot,' by the slanderous language Le used about the Salvatisn Army. Later the crowds increased until on Friday evening last the place was like a a bear garden. He used language that, was most blasphemous. He was olio of those customers who go from town to town pnaching and his following principally consisted of thepoor. He (the Chief)saw him on Satur- day ofterroon and cautioned him that if another repeti- tion took place on the Saturday night he would Le proceeded against. He promised Lint • (the speaker) that he would not address any more meetings. In pass- ing along in the direction of the Academy on Saturday evening t,o had great difficulty in getting through for it was blocked both ways. Quite a number of com- plain's had been received fro:n the inhabitants round about, owing to the man and the crowd. Mr. Crabtree bad said that his shop might as vsell have been closed all day. Ninety-eight per cent. of the crowd were in the males favour' and they were likely to have got noisy and riotous. Owing to one of his audience who desired to refute the statements en Satur- day evening, and wanted to go cn the platform, the platform was broken up. He went up to the defendant, who gave different names everywhere he went, and took him down to the Police Station, with a crowd of about 4,000 following him, who hooted and jeered the police aud gave cheers for " Pan " all the way down. Ho smuggled him out at the backdoor on his promise that he would not return to, Warrington and he went to Manchester. -On Monday he returned and sent word to the Chief Constable that he had Loan called to Warring- ton, but was not going to speak. During that day he went about with a crowd following him. On Monday evening he spoke on some land owned by Spencer Wall- head and Son in Bewsey-read, za here Thousands went to hear him. The whole neighbourhood was thrown into a state of disorder.He went away from there to his lodging- house in Buttermarket-street. On Saturday he had to sit by the fire whilst his shirt was being washed, and he was a thorough rascal. He had been a good deal of trouble this year in South Wales, and had been convicted of fraud at Birmingham. He represented himself to be .the son of a member of Parliament for South Australia and a nephew of Horatio Bottomley aid "John Bull." He had also stated in some places that he was paid by the National Sporting League to blackguard the
