THEFt OF -BRASS. METAL_ DEALER ACQF LTTED. _ _

At the Liverpool Intermediate Sessions on Tuesday, Alfred or John Barrow (23), collier, was charged with having stolen a number of brass steps, weighing 1701b5., the property Monks of Hall, and Company, Limited, of Warrington' during the month of April. M r.H.L. Riley (instructed by Messrs. Henry Greenall and Co.) prose- cuted, and evidence was given that the brass had been taken from the machinery at the company's works, and sold at a marine store. The prisoner denied the charge of theft, and said that be Lund the brass in an ashpit near the works. Ho was found guilty.—Joseph Matthews (59), metal dealer, was afterwards tried on a charge of feloniously receiving twelve brass steps on the 14th April from Alfred or John Barrow,the property of Monks, Half, and Company Limited. Mr. Riley was for the prosecution, and Mr. Madden' (instructed by Messis. Arthur Browne and Co.) for the defence. It appeired that the priscner Borrow was paid 4d. a lb. for the bras, which was broken up, and it was wilt to Glasgow the same day. The value of the metal as manufactured brass was ls. 6d. lb. For the defence it was submitted that the prisoner had no tll5- picion that the brass was stolen, and that a fair market price was given for it, namely, 4d. a lb., as against 5d., which prisoner's wife said he received for it. The reason it was sent away was that the prisoner was about to remove to, other premises, and therefore wanted to get rid of as much stock as possible. The prisoner and his wife gave every assistance to he police to find Barrow and to recover the brass. The jury found the prisoner not guilty. Mr. L. Riley said there were two other indictments, but he would offer no evidence on them. The prisoner was discharged.—The pri oner was senteueed to three rnenths' hard labour. CLERGYMAN DUPED. A CASE OF FALSE PRETENCES;

At the Burough Police Court on Tuesday, James Nolan, of no settled abode, was charged with obtaining sums of money by false pretences on Saturday last. He was further charged with similar offences on the 13th February last, and also on the 20th Ally last. He pleaded guilty to all three charges.—The Chief Constable stated that so far as the case on Saturday night last went it appeared that thortly before 11 o'clock prisoner went to the Rev. W. Bracecamp'sl house, and told them a pitiful tale about his wife and children, saying they were in tie very sore straits, and I asked him to relief them. Asa matter of fitat the wife and children were non-existent. Mr. Bracecamp,know- ing the prisoner, refused to give Lim anything. The prisoner then went to the house of the Rev. E. E. Peter's, curate st St. Paul's Chord), and told him that Mr. Bracecamp hid sent him for a grocery ticket. Mr. Peters after making wbat enquiries be could at such a late hour of ne night; gave him a ticket for s shilling's worth-of groceries to be obtained from a small provision- dealer in the town. The prisoner received the ticket and wilfully altered the is. into 4s. so as to obtain 4s. worth of groceries instead of one. He went to the dealer's and obt%ined 3s. Gd. worth of grocery., Prisoner asked the de-tler to allow him a bottle of "Bovril" for the remaining 6d. The dealer told him that they did not keep Bovril, so on asking for the Cd. in order to obta'n it elsawhere, it was returned to him: The facts in the other cases were similar. Prisoner had been going all over the town obtaining money in this way. He started last year. Prisoner had been once previously Lefore the court, on July 4th, 1906, to answer two cases of embezzling taoney.—Prisoner said ho was vety sorry tor what he hlcl dune. He was not lazy and vt as willing to work. Prisoner was sentenced to Ori3 months' imprisonment.

Warrington Borough Court.

MONDAY.—Before the M9yor (in the chair), Alderman Smethurst. Councillor Wilkinson and Messrs. S. Wright, W. Richardson, W. Roberts, S. Furness, H. Thornton and G. There was a good attendance on Sun-lay last, at the Wycliffe P.S.A. Mr. R. Atkinson presided,and mentioned the P.S.A. demonstrations in Belgium recently, while hundreds from this country went to take part in the proceedings which were characterised by great enthusiasm. Mr. Wardman spoke upon " Man " what he had done, N% hat he can do, and what he is destined to do. He maintained that all powers and gifts were given him by God, to use for noble purposes. Without Jesus Christ, all in and about man would come short, and all that was done for Christ and in Christ's name would have God's b'essing. Mr. C. Cotton read the lesson, and Mr. IVardman offered the prayer- The secretary said he had during the last week, wr:tten a letter of sympathy and condolence in their name as well as his own to Mr. John Blaclotirn (the founler of the P.S.A. movement) in the great loss he and his family had suffered in the sudden death of a son and brother,' aged 32, and who was a promising man in.business. Bold Street Wesleyan Church. Sunday School Anniversary.

The anniversary ser, ices in connection with the Bold- street Wesleyan Sunday School were held in tbe church lust Sunday, when the Rev. Joseph Manning of Preston, and formerly of this circuit, was the preacher both morning and evening. Special hymns were snug by the 'children and afforded striking testimony to • the excellent training of Mr. lolalik Hickman, who also presided at the organ. In the morning the rev, gentleman took his text from two passages of Scripture, 21st Revelation, sth verse, and tOlth Psalm,3oth verse. In the course of his sermon ha slid that it seemed to him th:it every Christian must las an optimist. He must have great faith in the future because he believed in God, and therefore be must believe in that new heaven aid new earth which God had promised. Think of all the great world movements that were opening up in the East and in our on hemisphere! It was spring time in the nations of the East to-day. Think of the Revolution in Turkey and the awakening of China and Japan ! Think of the new life that was dawning over Persia and over India ! In the West there were signs of new lire, for we were au akening to our rights and responsibilities. There was a quicken- ing scnsa of brotherhood, a keener sense of justice and a grottier love of mercy. There was mord pity, ?tore tenderness all over the world, and there was a higher standird of morality aolong-t the nations of the earth. Continuing, the preacher said that they re dised the importance of changing the heart and renewing men in righteousness and in love. "We bellow tint legislation can do something," he concluded," to bring in a new earth, but we believe that religion can do much more. Only the power of God can change the selfishness of human hearts, and teich man to love man as his brother, and to love God supremely first. Whatever else is needed to make this world what God means it to be . . . . the Sitn-Tay school is absolutely essential." During the service the choir sang "A Psalm of Life "very creditably. Mrs. A. Booth gave a powerful interpretation of" With Verdure Clad."

A flower service was held in theafternoon over which Mr. John Taylor presided. The children walked down the aisle singing a hymn Iva' at the rostrum handed their flawors to ladies, who in turn deposited them in a rack. The children then marched up the other aisle, and up to the g 'fiery. The effect was very pretty and .Mr. A. Meunfiald's address dealt with the value of flowers. He maintainei that flowers were one of God's best creations and he spokwon the text "Consider the lilies of the field how they grow . . . . even Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these." He said thst men was greater in the sight of God /than even the lilies. Mr. J. McArthur sane' " Cle,insing Fires," end Master Arthur Greenough "Angels ever bright and fair" with taste and expression. In the evening the preacher delivered an impressive sermon to a large congregation and dealt mainly with the sup-emacy of Christ. He took his text from the 2nd Phillipians 9v. and in the course of his discourse seid that Jesus Christ had won a place for himself which was absolutely unrivalled and absolutely unique Of the place which Christ cccupied in the political and social life of the world, the part which Christ played in the world's politics and legislation, hi,tory spoke in unmistakable terms. While it was perfectly true that there mere un-Christian elements in our social life and in our Kosent day civilisation, we must not fail to do juvice to what had been accomplished in the realm of our political and social life, through the inficence of Christianity. When Jesus was hanging upon the Cross there were in the Roman Empire at least sixty million slaves. To whom was the abolition of slavery die? Not to the ancient philosophers and moralists, for they taught that slavery was a national institution. The abolition of slavery was because Carist taught a great truth " that all men were brothers." He had raised worn,n from a position of degradation, from being the slave of man, and made her man's helpmate. Think of .the- way in tvhich the standard of morality had bent changed by the teaching of Jesus. Jesus Christ taught the truths of the broadest altreism. Those teachings were permeating slowly the whole of Oar civil life. There was scarcely an institution of our modern life that had not been changed by the influence of Jesus Christ. The influence of Jesus was not temporary. The influ- ence of Jesus was over cultured as well as uncaltssred people. A proof of the part that Christ oc.atpied in the thought and light of the world was that wecould not go to any past of Europe or any pa-t of America without finding here and there in every town, city, and in almost every village and h smlet a Temple or build-ng erected in the name of Jesus. Continuing the prertcher said that Christianity was divided into a multi( tide of sects. We held differdnt creels, we prayed in djferent languages yet we all bowed i's the aims of Jesus, it was the one name that tinted U 3. Conctuding the speaker maintained that it was the duty of all Christians to proclaim the name of Jesus, to be true to Him, to be loyal to Him, to live in His name, to raa.'re Him our ideal and to leara from him the true principle of life. The anthem by the choir was" Arm ! Soldiers of the Lord " and Mrs. Ernest Sanders interpreLel" Divine Redeemer " with much feeling. The colle,Aions amounted to RIO.

The TOWN CLERK sa;d Mr. Garside, who made the report in connection with the Lawson contract, had 'already been consulted in the matter and he approved the course decided upon by th 3 committee, in fa-t he almost. suggested it in the first instance. Councillor CRAIK also complained that Councillor Pernbertort's a, Milk) was not fair to the Water Com- mittee. He did not give advice but objected toevery- thing thrrt was brought forward. Cirmaillor PEMBEISTC(N said ha had offered sugges- tions, but he was insulted. Councillor WILKINSON said the reason for the scheme was th it at present they had not sufficient pressure to meet the demand, and it was thought advisable by the committer; that a larger main should 133 brought from Winwick to Lonford-street. Tney were in favour of & 21 inch main but Coancillor Pemberton thought 18 inches would be sulicieat. However, he did not oiler proof for his ststement. Whit did Warrington care about'a bit of money ?—(laughter)—and what did they care about a bit of money if they got the beat? .1t wtrs. the cheapest in the long run. Tne minut3s were approved. On the minutes of the General Purposes Committee .ppeared the following :—Tne Committee considered the report of the Water Committee on the High Level supply, and suggestions for the improvement thereof. It was resolved thst the recommend 'lions of the Com- mittee be approved, and that the Water Committee be authorized. to appoint two professional experts to report on the proposed scheme, and to give evidenee in support thereof before the Parliament3.ry Committee of the House. Councillor PEMBERTON moved that the- minute be referred back. The Council were being asked to con- firm the scheme before Mr. Hill had approved it. There were many things in Mr. Doss' report which he (Councillor Pemberton) did not think an expert would agree to. Winwick, which was a big customer of the Corporation, was sinking a well of its own. He con- tended that the scheme was based on wrong data and il some respects was impracticable. Were the Council going,to- adopt the scheme blind-fold and then employ Mr. Hill to report on it? The TOWN CLERK. said that when Alderman Evans brought it before the General Purposes Committee he said he only wanted an expression of approval of the scheme. That was all the resolution meant ; it was not adopting the scheme. The scheme. as amended bY Mr. Hill—if it were amended—would be submitted to two statutory meetings of the Council before it was adopted. After discussion, Councillor PEMI3EILTON withdrew his amendment on the unlerstanding that the experts' report would be submitted to the Water Committse before the scheme was brought up for adoption. The minutes were then approved.

THE ELECTRICLTY AND TRAMWAYS UNDER-

TAKINGS.

Alderman SMETHURST moved the aloption of the minntes of the Electricity an 1 Tramways Committee. and said at tirtt sight it might seem that the positi°n. of the electricity undertaking was not so satisfactory as previously. That, how ever, was nit the case. The dapartrnent had never Len in a stronger stste than at. present, the "costs of .production having been verY materinlly decreased dtu r ng the year, and the charges in force for lighting and power purposes and reduction or meter rents and discount put electricity well ahead of all „its competitors. With reference to the accounts of the Tramways Depnrtment for the year ending :Vat Nlarah, 1909. it avls a matter for congratulation that 11. net profit of £2,370 had been made, notwithstanding the concessions made in return fares. This was £209 le" than in the previous year land the decrease, as in tbe majority of other towns, was manly due to the state of trade. The profit would have been still less but for tt. further reduction in working F.xpenees, which were noss' almost at the low figure of 61d. per car mile. Alderman TIENNErr congratulated the Electricity an(' Tramways, Gas and Water Committees on the satis- factory results of the year's working. The net profits of the four departments amounted to nearly £1.8,00 and the gross profit- to about £55,010. Assuming the gross profit to be only £3ti,ooo it represented a dividend of 4 per cent on the total indebtedness of the Corpora- tion which amounted to £1,250,030 and included everl department. It wasp-it-Mental-1y gratifying in these daYst when we were so often. told that municipal enterptis° had failed. The minutes were approved. . •

lIIE CROWN PRINCE AND

PRINCESS OF SWEDEN.

- - to Pie Crown Prince and Princess of Sweden arrived in London on Tuesday on a visit th;47,e Duke and Duchess of Connaught, the parents of the Crown Princess. It is stated to die Crown Princess is the most domesticated of young Royal mothers, and according ell-know-i and popular Swedish writer, Mrs. Jensen, the Crown Princess insists upon th_u_!r children's clothes being made at home and under her direct supervision. Moreover, 4.erincess takes a great pride in the hands and feet of her little sons, personally cutting nails once a week, performing the delicate work of manicure and pedicure which is Usually lavished upon children of tender years.

ALDERMAN ARTHUR BENNETT.

I have been asked to supplement the tory of my father's life, and his reminiscences of my grandfather,by a brief account of my own; but I must confess that I respond to the request with some little diffidence, for my "life story" is, as yet, comparatively short, and my reminiscences do not go back far enough to make them of much value. But the Editor of the "Examiner" has always treated me with so much courtesy that I do not like

to say no ; and, if any of my recollections or experiences are of any interest to my native town, it is, of course, a pleasure to me to

record them. I was born at Tidy (or Tidal) Lane Farm on the 15th of November, 1862, but do not remember very much about those early days. We removed to Bruche (or Bruch, as it is more correctly spelt) in 1867; and, about this* time, I suffered from a serious illness. I had to lie upon a hard board, night and day, for seven months, and my life was at first despaired of. I gradually rallied, but had several minor recurrences of my ailment; and, on one occasion, kind old Dr. Hardy , whose long white beard and grave, benignant face I well recall, was per- forming a slight operation on me which made me suddenly cry out with pain. A faithful collie which happened to be in the room flew at the worthy doctor, bit him in the leg, and partially disabled him for several weeks. Eventually, I quite outgrew my ailment, and have since enjoyed almost unbroken health for many years. Dr. Hardy, I a may say in passing, resided in the house a part of which has long been occupied by "The Examiner." My education commenced at Padgate School,and I can plainly recollect old Mr. and Mrs. Birch, who were then the Headmaster and Headmistress. They lived at the picturesque white cottage which still stands just by the Church, and were typical of a class which was rapidly disappearing. The School was than comparatively small, and a curious old red thatched cottage, occupied by a hamper maker, stood upon the site of the last addition to the now extensive range of buildings. The Pinfold, mentioned by my father, was still in existence, but had fallen into disuse; and the Rev. A. A. Bridgman, who recently died at Lezayre, in the Isle of Man, at the advanced age of over 90, was the vicar. One of my earliest excitements was to watch' the railway wagons tipping the soil for the embankments leading up to Padgate Bridge. I can just remember the old jerry-house at the corner of Green-lane, and my native village was still a good deal as my father pictured it. I afterwards spent some time at the People's College, then a solitary building in " Slutcher's-lane," as Arpley-street was called, with fields all round it, and a pond and haystack just beyond the playground. The Barracks stood, of course, at the bottom of the lane, on the site of the Police Court ; and one of my most vivid rerollections is that of old Colonel Ireland Bla,ckburne drill- ing the Militia in the fields in front of the School. I can well remember the opening of Bank Park, and how my young imagination was impressed by a wonderful performance on the trapeze in the present Recreation Ground. I remember walking, afterwards, through the crowded rooms of the old mansion, and how my father drove us, when the darkness came, through the enormous crowds in Froghall- lane to see the fireworks. Mr. Charles Twiss was Headmaster of the People's Colle,Ye,as I still prefer to call it, at the time, with the present Master, Mr. Edward Wallington, as his second in command • and, although they caned me many a time, I shall always think of both of them with gratitude and with respect. Mr. T. Mee Pattison, who has since become famous as the writer of so many popular cantatas, used to come to the School, to teach us singing every Tuesday afternoon, and my love for many of our grand old national and patriotic songs dates from those weekly visits. One of my school-fellows was Mr. Edward Porritt, author of" The Unreformed House of Commons" and other well-known works, who has recently been appointed lecturer on English History at the Harvard University. In 1876 I went to Strathmore House School, Southport, which was kept by Mr. Francis R. Rigg, 8.A., a brother of the venerable Dr. J. H. Rigg, the famous Methodist theologian, statesman and expositor, who has lately passed away. Here I sat for two years next to Dr. Arthur Somervell, the dis- tinguished musical composer who annually visits the Warrington'Training College. He was then a very quiet and retiring youth whose principal hobby was to edit a small manuscript magazine, which he carefully wrote out himself on foreign notepaper. I remember that one of his brothers was, at this time, busily engaged in waging doughty war against the Thirlmere Water Scheme. The Rev. Stanley Rogers, the well-known Liverpool minister, and son of the Rev. J-. J. Guinness Rogers, was another of my school- mates, and had won the reputation of a "swot," as the schoolboys most irreverently termed the lads who took a serious interest in their studies and regarded -them as of at lest as much importance as their cricket. The railway from Southport to Preston was being made at the time, and robbed us of a corner of our cricket field. During my residence at Strathmore House I attended the beautiful Mornington-road Chapel, and sat for two years under several very famous preachers, including the Rev. F. W. Macdonald, who was afterwards President of the Conference; was twice enthralled by Morley Punshon's magic eloquence; and once heard Dr. Osborne hold forth for an hour and thirty-five minutes. I can still fancy I can overhear Mrs. Rigg, who sat immediately behind me, murmuring to her learned husband, in disgusted tones, that " the dinner would be quite spoilt." It has always been a source of some regret to me that I never' attended our grand old

===== 0002601_19090612_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
MY LIFE STORY.

Boteler Grammar School, but I have many pleasant memories of my life at Strathmore House. Some of my friends wanted me to go to Cambridge, but I did not welcome the idea at the time, and, although I have some- times since regretted this, upon the whole, I think that all was ordered for the best. "There's a divinity that shapes our elide, Rough-hew them how we will ;" and, after all, I doubt if there is any school so useful or instructive as the school of life. Moreover, circumstances accidently contri- buted to lead me into paths which have been singularly happy and congenial. - As my father mentioned last week, I was descended, on my mother's side; from the Ocklestons, a Cheshire family sonic of whom were tanners, and my father had a,wish that I should follow this career; but there were one or two difficulties in the *.Way, and, asl luck would have it, at this very juncture, he chanced to hear that there was a vacancy in the office of Messrs. Joseph Davies, Voisey • and Davies, of Bewsey Chambers, Warring- ton, and asked me if I thought that I should like to be an accountant. I scarcely knew what an accountant was in those days, but I had left school, and was very anxious to get to business, so I aciuiesced in the sugges- tion, and, in 1878, was articled to the firni. Alderman Davies was eminent in his pro- fession, having for some years acted as Presi- dent of the Society of Accountants in England and Wales; and, whilst I was serving my articles, he took a leading part in the negotia- tions which resulted in the amalgamation of all the existing Societies of Accountants and the granting of the Chatter in the year 1880. I was one of the first articled pupils to be registered, and to join the now tremendous roll of Chartered Accountants by examina- tion. It is a rather curious coincidence that my friends, Mr. Richard Le Gallienne and Mr. James A. Welch, should both have commenced their careers in the offices of Chartered Accountants, and Mr.Le Gallienne has criticised the avocation most unmercifully in "Young Lives"; but I do not know that I have ever regretted my choice. The profession has steadily increased, since then, in public con- fidence and favour. I cannot imagine any better business training; nor do I "think,

_ upon the whole, that there is any profession which renders truer service to the public.

frilie-Bill which has just been framed to make registration compulsory, and to eventually prevent any unqualified accountant from practising, should pass, there is not any doubt that the profession has a still greater future in front of it. I may add that Mr. Alderman Forshaw was a leading member of the office staff when first I went to Bewsey Chambers, and that I laughingly predicted, many a time, that he would eventually be Mayor of Warrington, like Mr. Davies; and it is a peculiar pleasure to me that my prophecy should have been fulfilled in a year which is to be distinguished by such an exceptional honour as a visit from the King. But the activities of Alderman Davies were not by any means confined to his pro- fession. The man who lives entirely for his business is a poor creature, and Mr. Davies took a very prominent part in public life. He even dabbled in literature, and, although he once wrote to a client that he was "trying to rub off my romance upon the grindstone of accountancy," he really fostered it. I have always been a great reader, and have been afflicted with the "scribbling mania" almost from my infancy; and somebody happened to tell Mr. Davies that I had perpetrated a novel. A frightfully crude and melodramatic production it was, but he asked me to allow him to read it; and, doubtless to encourage me in my literary tastes, he invited me, in the summer of 1879, to accompany himself and his son Harry upon a tour in Norway. A happier holiday I never spent. The grandeur of those mighty mountains and those sombre fiords impressed my young imagination greatly, and fanned my literary aspirations into a-flame. I have many happy recollections of the journey; of Mr. Davies's unfailing humour and bonhomie; of the majestic beauty of the scenery; and of the numerous incidents which diversified the tour. One of the most agreeable of these

recollections concerns a certain morning when we accidentally breakfasted at Bergen with the great Norwegian violihist, 'Ole Bull, and his young wife. Lean still remem- ber his fine old face, his long white locks, his kindly eyes and ample brow, and can almost hear the very accents in which. he told my good old Tory friend and principal that "Lord Beaconsfield would go up like a rocket and come down like the stick."

I kept a record of the holiday, and, on my return, encouraged by the kindly words of Mr. Davies, I published my first book, a little thin red volume entitled "Travels in Norway; or, Three Weeks in 'the Land of the Midnight Sun.' " It was printed by Mr. Joseph Truelove, who kept a bookseller's shop in Horsemarket-street, and has since achieved considerable reputation as a London publisher and as the proprietor of. three of the most popular London Hotels. lam not altogether sorry that the book is out of print. I, may add that I was indebted to Mr. Davies for my first visit to London and to the Theatre. We stayed in Ely Place, Holborn. I remember that he took me specially to show me Goldsmith's grave, and, in the evening, accompanied me to the Lyceum, to see Henry Irving and Ellen Terry in "Hamlet." I could scarcely have made a better beginning., Mr. Davies was also good enough, a little later, to stimulate my,literary aspirations by reprinting, at his own expense, one of my earliest attempts at poetry, some lines entitled "Albion," which had appeared in" The Examiner " ; and to ask me to collect and preserve for him the numerous allusions to his public work which were then appearing in the newspapers. This I did in two huge scrap books, and it is not too much to say that my interest in things municipal dates from those five delightful years at Bewsey Chambers. During my articles, I took the keenest possible interest in athletics and spent scores of hours at the old Baths; and it was a favourite custom with myself and the other two articled clerks (Mr. Harold Ayrton Bankes and Mr. Charles Dutton Parkinson) to row down the river to. Walton Arches in the dinner hour. kalso took much interest, at this time, in cricket and football, and for some years acted as Secretary of the famous " Padgate Excelsior Club." I entertained a vague idea, then, that in the ancient motto," Mens sana in corpore ,sano," might be found the key to all our troubles and perplexities; and, thinking it was possible to blend literature and football in appropriate propor- tions, established a library in connection with the Club. I must confess that it was rather a failure. But, if the mem- bers did not blossom put, .as I had hoped, into philosophers and poets, they became exceedingly redoubtable footballers. We gradually attracted the best of the local players and soon became serious rivals to the Warrington team. Amongst our members was the famous "Tommy Barnes," who was once the hero of the North, Tom Pemberton and Mr. J. E. Warren, whose work for foot- ball and athletics generally is recognized and honoured far beyond the limits of their native place. Eventually the old town club was

===== 0002601_19090612_art0077.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
.7

===== 0002601_19090612_art0078.txt | included=true | included =====
ignominiously beaten by the village team, and an amalgamation was arranged which paved the way for all the triumphs Warrington has since so brilliantly won. I .believe that the old Excelsior flag is still engraved upon the Club notepaper. I may add that I made the acquaintance of my good friends, Alderman Tinnion and Mr.W. H. J. Jenkinston the football field. I first met the lather in a field behind Fairfield Hall, on the very day of the first Bruche fire; and, although I was never able to aohieve dis- tinction myself, I was just as proud of the prowess of the good old Club as anybody else. But it was local prowess then, and we played for the game and not for the gate. it was during this period that we formed the famous old Padgate Wesleyan Mutual Improvement Society. Here, with my friend Mr. Councillor J. J. Nield (of whose serious illness I have heard with so much pain), I first began to advocate the principles of Imperial Unity and Tariff Reform. Here Mr. Charles Plinston, the able Speaker of the Parliamentary Debating Society, Mr. Theodore Craig London, and Mr. Henry Hewitt Dutton moved many d crowded meeting by their youthful eloquence; and I and many of my fellow-townsmen owe a lasting debt of gratitude to this and to the various other societies of a similar character which flourished at that time. I was member for " Orkney and Shetland" in the first "Local Parliament," but never ventured to open my mouth in it; and I have never quite got over the disgust which filled me when the Conservative Party, annoyed by some rash utterance of Mr. Henry Whitham, got up one night in a body, and abjectly left the room, thus break- ing up an institution which had shown so many signs of life and vigour, and which might have played so prominent a part in the affairs of the old town.

About this time I had begun to send an • occasional contribution to the "Young Folks Paper." I took it regularly for many years, add a regiment of its green volumes occu- pies an honoured place upon my study shelves. It would be interesting to learn how many of our modern writers first found recognition in its kindly pages. Amongst the amateur contributors I recol- lect the names of A. C. Harmsworth, C. A. Pearson, Israel Zangwill, B. Murray Gilchrist, A. ' St. John Adcock, Arthur Edward Waite, and many more. Robert Louis Stevenson wrote some of his best romances for its lucky troop of youthful readers, and to William Sharp, its literary editor, who afterwards became illustrious as "Fiona Macleod," I owed my earliest definite encouragement in literature and dedicated my first book of verse. I have already incidentally alluded to my friends Mr. Theodore Craig London, Mr. Richard Le. Gallienne and Mr. James A. Welch. Mr. London was then living at Padgate, and through him I made the acquaintance of the youthful' poet who has since achieved so much distinction and the young comedian whose name is now a house- hold word. With Mr. London and Mr. E. James Hall, I attended the meetings of "The Book-Lovers' Club," which had been founded by Mr. Le Gallienne, and periodically met in Birkenhead at the houses of the local "Squires and Dames`of Books and Pens," as Mr. Le Gallienne picturesquely called us. How vividly those "nights ambrosial" come back to me, especially that memorable even- ing when we said good-bye to " Jimmy " Welch as he left us to accept his first engage- ment on the stage! My articles had now expired, and, in 1882,1 became a fully qualified Chartered Account- ant. I almost succeeded in obtaining a berth in London, with one of the biggest firms in the City, who, however, notwithstanding their great reputation, turned out to be financially unsound, and had to fly to Australia because they could not find the funds to meet the unclaimed dividends appropriated by the Board of Trade upon the passing of the present Bankruptcy Act. It was a lucky miss for me, and, shortly afterwards, Mr. Lewis Voisey, who was one of the ablest accountants I have ever met, opened an office in Manchester and invited me to take charge of it.l spent two years with him in Cottonopolis, and, afterwards, a year or so in Liverpool, and a further couple of years in Warrington; and, in 1888, I started business for myself right in the centre of the town, in an office which had previously been occupied by Mr. H. Lindon Riley, who was then a solicitor, and where, above the roar of Market Gate, I have spent the best part of my daily life for over twenty years. I have many interesting memories of Market Gate, but I must not be tempted to indulge in them. Nor have I space to tell the tale of" Sunrise" and "The Dawn," and of the numerous historical events and strange experiences to which my twenty years of amateur journalism introduced me. With the story of my public life my fellow-townsfolk are sufficiently familiar, and of my private life it is enough to say that, in 1895, I was married to the eldest ,daughter of the late Mr. Edwin Richardson; that we spent ten happy years at "Morning- side," right " in the shadow of the spire" which Joseph Davies helped to build; and that, eventually, I was drawn back to my native heath again, where I am now residing, with my wife and boy, in the old house at Paddington which, curiously enough, adjoins my father's birthplace. It was, for many years, the home of William Wilson, the distinguished botanist, and here I can feast my eyes on chestnut blooms instead of chimneys, and nightly see the old Town Clock, amid the soft green foliage, shining like a moon above the place I love.

ECZEMA ATTACKS WHOLE FAMILY.

Mother, Father, and 5 Children cured by

Zam-Buk.

Further remarkable proof of Zam-Buk's unrivalled akin-curing powers is displayed in the case of a family of seven—all of whom have been cured of severe '! catching" eczema that resisted all other treatments. A" Newark Herald "representative called upon Mrs. C. J. Tinkler at 12, Elgin Place, Newark. "What you have heard is quite true," said Mrs. rinkler, in reply to the reporter's first query. We have all been wonderfully cured of terrible eczema by Zam-Buk. I ' began with the disease first about a year ago, and I had been suffering only a little time when my husband and all our five children—Ellen (16), Florence (13), George (10), Willie (7), and Gertie (4)—caught the disease, and soon we were all suffering severely. The skin itched fearfully, and was very hot with inflammation.

" We gave the doctor's treatment a good trial, and then had to give it up, as we all seemed worse. I wag almost at my wits' end, for besides suffering severely myself, I had to look after my husband and the children, who 'were practically helpless, and cried bitterly with the constant pain. " For, many months our hopes of ever getting better grew fainter and fainter, until my husband came in one day and joyfully. showed me an account of how Zam-Buk had tured a Bristol family of eczema. We at once got a supply of Zam-Buk, which we all used straight away. Before we retired at night we had got an ease from the pain such as we had not known for months. By morning there was further improvement, but with constant dressings of Zam- Buk the awful itching and burning disease was got under, and one by one the children were cured. "My husband and I also persevered with Zam-Buk, and our skin, and that of every member a our family, is now perfectly healthy."

beyond. I seem to know, and yet I do not know. You, Paddy, with your powers of swift perception, will not fail to understand me. You will read already in this book of an amazing destiny.. I say that they left me in this splendid hall, and that for many minutes I had no coM- panion there. The house Itself spoke both of occupation and -of ceremony. I perceived footmen about the table in the dining-room, whose door opened to the right of the great fireplace where the logs blazed brightly. The landing above echoed the voices of maids, and, upon that, another voice which caused my heart to leap as though one had spoken to me from the grave. When a chime of bells echoed musically in the heights cf the dome, I understood that they were ringing the dressing-bell, and remembered with some consternation that I had come to the château "as I was." But the thought passed, away as swiftly as car and train could carry me. If anything disturbed my serenity, it was the absence of the Marquis. He knew of my arrival, and should, I thought, have wel- comed me the sooner. But the minutes passed, and still he did not come; and one by one the old phantoms swarmed up to tor- ment me. If she were not here, after all! If a trick had been played me! Inconceivable, and yet how •real to a man who had suffered so much.

It was odd, Paddy, but all memory of the tragic days we. have lived through passed utterly from my recollection in that house. No longer did I care what the worid had said of Mimi or what it would say 'to-morrow. That awful night, when I stood upon the threshold of my little house to gape upon a dead man's body and to know that my wife had left me; that had been 'wiped out' of my calendar as though a hand of mercy reviewed the page. The house in which I stood, the great names I had heard, Mimi's presence at the chateau—with what woeful reiteration did 1 not repeat the harassing questions to which I had no answer. She knew that I was here and yet she did liot come to me! How my heart rank at that! What an abasement of love and faith and how swift a repentance!.

'She conies at last—l hear the patter of feet on the stairs above —so gentle that it might be the south wind brushing the cheeks of a rose. What a moment to live as I turn about to spy it sweet apparition on the stairs, to watch a girlish figure descend them one by one, to say that she is my Mimi, and yet to remain almost unbelieving. Stand so far with me, Paddy, but then shad you turn your MS away. There are things said and done be- tween two who love which are holy in their sanctity and God-given in their secrecy. Were it otherwise I could no more tell you what befell us in that instant of greeting than I could speak the dreams which the lightest hours of sleep have given me. Was it not enough that I caught her in my arms and that kisses forbade her to speak at all? Are there not hours of living so precious that they would remain heaven hough it were hell afterwards to all eternity? And such an hour was that at the Château of Bougival—when Mimi ran down the great staircase to my embrace, and my lips sealed her sweet confession. You will remember that she wore a -trim black dress when she was with us at 'Tamp- stead, made cleverly as the French make all I these things ; but a little overmuch in the fashion of the nunneries. I recoilect that we chaffed her about it, and that the Chevalier would have gone out to get a Franciscan robe to match it; while old. Georges Oleander was all for singing the office, especially that part of it whkh coun- sels the giving of alms to the needy. This dress and the pretty picture it en- shrined have stood during all these weary days for my image of Mimi as I should find her at last and take her again to my house. But it was not to be. The Château of Bougi- val has dealt too well by its prisoner. No little girl of the atelier and the mountain de- scended that proud staircase to my embrace. In her place I found a stately figure of .Paris, gowned and dining; a Mimi robed in silk and chiffon, with a. gift of jewels about her white neck and a sparkle of diamonds upon her arms. Oh, iend the grace and shyness of her, as though she were half afraid, but wholly sure of her reception! And here is the won- der. She spoke not at ail of the things I had expected to. hear from her lips. She told me nothing of the night of crime and flight : nothing of the days intervening ' • no story of her coming to this house—but happy in my arms she laughed at my perplexities and asked me if I would have her otherwise. And, Paddy, I knew not what to say to. her. She is changed as it were in a twinkiing. My. quick perception could not put the•fact aside. She has come into her inheritance—she Mimi of the Butte no longer and never will be again. Let me try to tell you ,of the talk which passed between us as we stood together in the ball and cared less than nothing though ail France had been listening. It will not be to write down the sighs and sounds of a lover's meeting—you, Paddy, of all men care nothing for those since you met the widow at I Ostend; but I would wish to tell you how cleverly this mere child kept. even from me, the things she had been charged to hold sacred, and how even my persistency could not shake her. And first, of our meeting in the house at all.

"The Marquis sent for me, Mimi," said I; "I looked to find him here."

"And you found me, Monsieur Henry--"

"Monsieur Henry! Am I that to you, Mimi? Is it the Mimi of the Lapin Agit again? Let me look into your eyes—iet me see why you are changed.'" "I am not changed," she rejoined very sweetly, "but I am very happy, mon marl." "How long have you been in this house, Mimi?"

She tried to think. An impaired memory is among the first fruits of all that as befallen her. _

-mes enfants, how long have I been here? There would be nights and days—long nights and (tap. Then Maclaine came and I was happy. She will tell you, Harry—she can remember how long I have been here." "Did the Marquis bring you, then? Did he discover you at the villa?" She shuddered ; a spasm of pain crossed her face. • I regretted that I had spoken of such a place at all. "Bedotte went down to the river," she said presently, her mind gathering the threads one by one, "I was alone and afraid. Alors, the woman Marie is there and I think of her. Alt, mes enfants! She comes to my bedroom and begins to tell me the things I heard long ago, when I was a little child and ran away to the woods. I think she was irre,. mon mart—and I laughed at her. Then I saw that she was no longer the Madame Marie who had frightened me. She prayed and mumbled and wept. Oh, it was droll, for she seemed to think I was a baby again and would have sung me to the sleep. But I crept away, and then she told Inc to go. It was me:battle to hear that. Go,' she said, and she tumbled to the door and held it wide open—and the black house was there and the men to kill me. I was afraid to go, and I told her so, and then she wept again and sat by the fire rocking herself as a baby. CHAPTER XLI.

[ln which 'Henry Gastonard receives a sum, -mons from the Marquis do Saint Faun] Château of Bougival. November Bth, 1905. near Mr. Gastonard, The obligation of silence Which recent events have imposed upon me is the more deserving of apology as it is the less possible of explanation. May I beg of you to believe that all which has been done, or contemplated, is such as would appeal to any man of honour, and particularly to one who has shown such gifts of prudence and self-restraint as you have done these latter days?

The story of Madame Gastonard's infancy is not one which may be written circumstan- tially, even for you, Monsieur. But the pages which are missing will be supplied by your knowledge end experience of the lkorld and of men, and will not be regretted be- cause of that knowledge. Great names ar6 implicated, and particularly the name of a noble woman, who has suffered much, and yet must suffer. I beg you, in the name of womanhood, to bear this fact in mind from the beginning. For the rest, I am content that your judg- ment shall decide what its to be told to the world and what concealed. The rest is your own—an inheritance of a destiny once dc• creed and irrevocable.

Do me the honour. I beg of you, to conics to the Château de Bougival without delay, there to hear from Madame Gastonarcfs own lips both the 'story of these recent days as she alone may tell it, and that other story, which will be told for the first time to any man by, Yours, with cordial eFteem, _

GASPARD DE SAINT FAUR.

CHAPTER, XLIT.

[Paddy O'Connell hears that ho may leave London, and is invited to take the first train to Paris.] Chateau (le 13ougival, near Paris. November 15th, 1905. Dear Palidy,—Please to pack that strolls bag of yours, and to come to u. mediately. You will get a train front Paris to St. Germain, and I will send a motor there to meet you. But be sure to wire, for Mimi says you are more likely to send your telegram from this house than from London. Oh, Paddy: Paddy—what a day. what news, what happiness I ant here in this old château, and Mimi is at .my feet while write to you. We have before its the great park of Bougival ; there is warm sunshine though the month is November; the trees are bare and leafless, but they 'show us the shining river, and it shall wing our message to the frielids who love us. For thirty7stx hours I have hardly • let Mimi escape from my arms, but to-day she bids me write to you; and I obey, even as she who watches me with such a story of love and gratitude it her childish eyes.

non- lm-

I have found her—the dreadful days will come no more: already we have learned to believe that yesterday was not, and that to- day is eternal. So much is to be told imme- diately ; but or the rest your own perception shall 'tell you, and you ;done, when you come to BouAival. And you will come without do= lay. Trains shall be too slow for you; the sea shall provoke you; the .inventions of man imprison those kindly thoughts you would speed to us. As you stood beside us in the hour of darkness, so now will you stand with us in the light—the first and best and biggest-hearted of our friends. So, ' I repeat, let nothing prevent you, but come, for we weary for you. • It was on Wednesday afternoon that I re- ceived a letter from the Marquis de Saint Faur inviting me to this place. There was little in it that would interest you beyond the invitation which it contained, and you may imagine with what haste I set out at its com- mands. Oh, be sure, I had some dim percep- tion of the truth, or I would not have been content to rest in that lonely 'hotel, as I did, and to suffer patiently the mysteries which crowded upon me. Mimi was well, I said, and had the best of reasons for her silence. To-day I know it was the truth. if you ever come to. understand, Paddy—in which case you _will be the one man in all Europe ' Nvho will share the great truth with me— then your own shrewd common-sense will have written the story for you. My lips are sealed; I am content that they should be sealed. For is not Mimi at my feet while I write, and may not I stoop to kiss her rosy checks when I will?

1 set out for Bougival on Wednesday night, then, and in 20 minutes was at its gates. To my inquiry whether the Marquis owned the . Chateau, the people about gave an evasive answer. Some seemed to think that he (lid; others spoke of a foreign tenancy, chiefly by that beautiful but notorious woman the Prin- cess Helene of Ilidze, and her cousin the Duchess de Bourg. Both these ladies had been with Saint Fair when:l met him at the Ritz Hotel in Paris; and from all that gos- sip says, it was no surprise to me that one of them, at any rate, should be found at Bougi- val. But of this I had little time to speca- late, for 1 set out for the Chateau within half an hour of receiving the Marquis's letter, and was at the gates exactly at seven o'clock on Wednesday evening. I knew that it NV ati seven o'clock, for bells chimed as my car raced up the long avenue from the lodge, and the great clock above the stables was still telling the hour when a but- ler opened the door to me and invited me within. That the servants expected me it was not possible to doubt. Neither my name nor my business was asked, but being con- ducted directly into the great hall of the chateau, footmen relieved me of my wraps and assured me that Monsieur le Marquis i-hould at once be informed of my arrival. And so they left me in that stately place; and tor the first time since the letter came to me, I could ask myself the, meaning both of it and of this bewildering sequel. Imagine a vast apartment, Paddy, domed above and built almost entirely of marble. There were mosaics in gold for the frieze and paintings above, such paintings as Vernet made for the ceilings of the Louvre, but skil- fully -adapted to a vast and ornate concavity. A wide staircase glowing with a crimson car- pet boasts five caryatides to bear its burdens. The chimney is a masterpiece by an unl-nown artist—a colossal • structure -protruding cen- taurs far into the room and pillared with Jas.- per and chalcedony. Above there is a great picture of Turenne, the Turenne of the .fables and the wars, mounted. and riding as a Mar- shal-General of France. Other portraits also of soldiers adorn the place, but there is one of the Empress Josephine, painted, I imagine, during the stormy days of St. Cloud, and cleverly reflective of that turbulent time, whicn is a masterpiece beyond all question. As to the furniture, it is sparse but very beautiful. I note a clock with the Graces which could not be bettered at Fontainebleau. • A massive bureau is in the finest style of that magnificent Bouhl whom posterity has imi- tated and derided. A brief glance says that this apartment stands as an atrium to a house of princes. The manner of it. the size of it, that indefinable atmosphere which the ages create could never mislead. I am in the house of an aristocrat, and he has summoned me to find Mimi there. So much is plain from the beginning. but the event which carried my beloved wife to such a plaee, which sealed her lips while she resided there, and brings me to her as a very suppliant, is dwelt upon almost with reluct- ance, stands, it may be, almost as a shadow

We arrive when all this has happened, but our knowledge of human nature is not deep enough to read the riddle aright. All the world followed her flight from the Austrian Court with the singer Monterez; her amazing escapades at Geneva have newer been concealed:• her sub- sequent appearance in Paris and friendship with the Marquis de Saint Faur have long ceased to amuse as gossip. _ .

Tho Emperor Franz Joseph is the greatest man in the world, she thinks, and Anatole France the next to him. Paris, she declares, is being spoiled by the Socia- lists. The new Frenchman is well repre- sented by the ugly steam trams which dis- figure his streets. Religion is fashionable only among monarchists, and will profit eventually by their conservative traditions. Sentiment is departing from France; it would be a good thing if a king were chosen and murdered—for that would revive senti- ment. As for art, the moderns are gelling as good as they deserve, and if they desire better, they should hang the picture-dealers. She herself has recently discovered Corfu and means to build a vane there. She ;declares it to be the finest climate in the world—and you can only play roulette when the sun is shining. All this, mind you, in a mood most frivolous; by no means embarrassed, casting sm sweet iles about her, and stroking little Mimi's hand from time to time ns though a new, pet had come 'into her house and must be made much of.

I could but make a hazard at the dinner-table and leSs than a hazard afterwards. A game of billiards with the Mar- quis, a little gallantry played by the obliging Duchesse—and then to bed. And through it all my dear wife acting a part to perfection.,

HARRY CASTONARD.

cIIAPTER XLIII.

[Martha Warrington, being retitrned to her . home, receives there a , letter from the Château of Bougival.] Château de Bougival. -November 19th, 1905. Dear Mrs. Warrington,- -I have to toll you that I have kit the horse-riders of Hamp- stead and am come to this house, which is a palace not far from Paris, and a.; comfort- able quarters as Paddy O'Cutineli has 'lighted upon these many days. Should you wish to write to me, address the letter es above, thought • you might put "care of the Marquis de Saint Faur,'• who is one of my friends, and a very noble gentleman. • Well, I. arrived here after the blazes of a journey, and not knowing at all why Henry had sent for me. At first I thought it would be some -news which he could not well put on paper, and after that I thought it wouldn't. Bougi val., I would tell you, is a stretch of park-land on the river near Paris, lying under the height of St. Germain, which you will have heard of in the French hooka your husband forbids you to read. The train toot me to the station of the place, and a Fine motor-ear from there the house; so, you see, I was made much e!' and mighty pleased with myself when I set foot in what the French call "the vestibule." 'Ti; true that the footmen—and the Mar- quis must have half a hundred of • them in this place—speak the French. tongue poorly, and that I had to fall back on my Sasso- nach ; hut we must allow something to igno- rance, and that is as cheap an article in France a!, in England. Any way, I couldn't be angry in so tine a house; and, being for- tunate enough to arrive about half-past seven o'clock of the evening, they conducted me to a bedroom as ,big as a church, and sent as many 'men to wait upon me as though I had km,,en an Emperor. This put Paddy in a dilemma, for, notwithstanding that his forefathers were kings in their own coun- tries, he can't suffer another man to put a shirt on his back, nor is he hanny when a couple of flunkeys would see., his head in the washtub.. _ .

You must know that Harry wrote me a long letter inviting me to this house, and telling me nothing at all, except that his wife was here. I'd suspected it all along, though hearing the name of the place for the first time; .and now., says I, the truth is coming out at last. 'Tis some great man or woman in Paris .who has been after hunting the child, and that poor Count d'Antoine who was murdered, at Hampstead was bio his or her ambassador:. I'd have been worss than a fool not to have guessed as much aftc: receiving Harry's letter and reading the papers—though God forbid I should come to believe any newspaper at •my time of life. For all that, I know a fox when I see one; and what I say is this, that Mimi has aristo- cratic friends in Paris, and there were rogues who got news of those friends, and set out to blackmail them. I'll swear as much against all the justices. Ms a vulgar crime, after all, and the truth. will make it no better.

- This I must tell you to begin with, for 'Us a different kind of story which must come after. The last paragraph of this letter left me in the dressing-room at the château—and that's no place for a lady. I'd been there about five minutes, I suppose, when Harry came roaring in, and hailed me with a hunt- ing cry you could have heard away in Paris. Faith, the man had lost his head entirely, and when he had kissed me on both cheeks' as the Frenchmen do, he. rolled me over and over on the bed—though I had a clean shirt on my back—and treated me worse than any terrier. 'Twits "Paddy, you brick!" and "Paddy, such news ! " and "She's here, in the hour, Paddy !" until my ears were bursting with it. Not a word would he let me speak, though I had k king's message to bear him, and when I got down to the draw- ing-room at last my collar was up to my ears and my white choker on top of it. A fine figure of a man I must have looked among such company and the Marquis bowing to me „pntil his nose almost touched his boots, and Madame the Princess flashing a pair of eyes nearly as black and as wicked as your own. But there I was, and I had to make the best of it, and not a man or a ,woman would I notice until I had caught the little Greuze girl in my arms and squeezed her uptil she hollered.

Well, they. were all amused at -this, and Madame, the Princess Helene, who is one of my friends, and a great lady in Paris, she took . mv arm and led me off to the dinner. table. I had the Duel-168e upon one side of me and the Princess upon the other; and faith, says • I, what a tale to tell at Glenda- lough. Such gold and silver plate, Martha ; such glass. such paintings ! Ny neck aches this very day for staring up to the ceilings to look at the gods and goddesses (lancing in the hayfields, and caring no more for the liquor down below than the archbishop at a teetotalers meeting. What with these bare- leFged beauties and the great ladies and the witty talk, bedad, I could have been con- tent • to stop at the table a month, and to have thought twice about leaving it then. But this is to speak of Paddy O'Ccinnell, and you, woman-like, will be all for hearing of Madame Mimi. How did she bear her- self, you ask? • The little colleen out of the cafés and the circus—how did she carry her- self in such, a place? Why, no grand dame born to it could have done better. Eveh my friend the Marquis told rue so afterwards ; while, as for the Princess, she couldn't like the child better if she were her own daugh- ter. Not one of them that does not spoil and pet her, believe me. I listen to their talk, and ii thousand wild thoughts run in my- head. What is Mimi to them? Why did they send to London for her? Vow comes it that ruffians Niould have blackmailed them? You are s discreet little bodyf and, you will

llUlMpttilt u.lll/0 using ; IJIAL you'll think about it very much, Martha, as I am • thinking about it this minute, and saying I'd be a fool not to guess the truth ef it. And if my suppositions are true, is the child's be- haviour a wonder to be gaped at? You will be the first to say "no" to that. She has the blood of nobles in her veins, and what matter jot if the bend sinister is written across her story? Let the story go where it will. I would be proud of it if-I were Harry—and so much I have told him to-day..

Write to me • as soon as you can--when your husband is, at his sermons should be a good opportunity—and tell me how far I am right. My own programme is uncertain. This lovely November weather makes life well enough in the glorious park about this château, and a sweet time we are having, of it. The , Mar- quis plays olf very well, and I am winning money of him. Mimi and Harry are nearly always on horseback, for he is teaching her to ride, and a willing pupil he has *found. What is troubling them is the approaching trial of the man Bedotte, who, it seems, was Count d'Antoine's valet, and is to be tried at St. Germain for the murder of the old woman Marie. I fear the public, which laps up a scandalous story ,as a dog takes water, will insist upon much being told which otherwise should be concealed. But we shall have the judge with us, for where is the lawyer born who could write unkind things about that beautiful woman the Princess Helene?

Rely upon me, in any case, to send you all the news. Harry is fretting 'still for his for- tune, though Mimi, I bear, is to have two thousand a year' from the Marquis. He says he means to accept an offer from the editor of the "Daily Bulletin" to become Paris cor- respondent at a stiffish sum, and I do believe he'll carry out the threat. In Which case they sill have an apartment in Paris and a villa at Fontainebleau which he discovered the other clay when in his motor-car.

_ I'll be a lonely man. then, Martha, and glad to see you sometimes. Should the "Society for the Suppression of Human Emotions " de- termine to hold its meetings in London, you'll be going there and may menage to let me know. In which case, ro accident might find me in the great Metropolis also. Meanwhile, with my kind regards, believe me, dear Mrs. Warrington, yours faithfully, PADDY O'CONNELL. (To be Concluded.)

OPEN AIR POLITICS.

THE BUDGET A VINDICATION OF FREE TRADE FINANCE.

Mr. Howes and the Lusitania,

There was an attendance of upwards of 1,000 at another of the series of open-air meetings promoted by the Warrington Progressive Labour League, which was held at Bridge-foot (near Arpley Cannons) on 'Monday evening. The - principal speaker was Mr. SAMUEL CLEAVE, of the Free Trade Union, who held the keen attention of the audience by the lucid and convincing way in which be exposed tariff reform fallacies and showed the advantages of Free Trade. Mr. E. Wynne- Jones (President of the League) presided and was supported by Messrs. J. Howes, J. Elwell, S.VVright ; J. A. J. Morton and J. Howes, jun. (hon. secretaries). The Cir A lasi AN referred to the glorious victory of Mr. Crosfield at the last general election, and said the Progressive Labour League had to take its part in nutititaining the great majority, and if possible increasine• it. He did not think the publics bad any idea of the prohibitory nature and scope of the proposals of the Tariff Retormers. Sometimes they said they. were only: going to. tax foodstuffs, but Mr. Chamberlain had we itien to someone to say that they were going to tax foreign graniee • someone else had said they were going to tax foreign dates and cement, and Mr. Wyndham bad told us that they were goiug to tax wood. Where they would finish it was impossible to say, but it could be takt-n as a fact that every article subjected to the tariff would be dearer immediately. The speaker quoted from a speech by Mr. Chamberlain in the old days, in which he said that Protection would increase the incomes of owners of great estates, swell the profits of the capitalists who were fortunate enough to engage in the best Protected industries, but it would lessen the total production of this country, diminish the rate of wages, and raise the price of every necessary of life. Tariff Reform, continued the Chairman' would make the rich richer and the poor poorer. The great bulk ofg loreign manufactures introduced in this country con- sisted of material and plant that were used in producing employment for British workmen. He ventured to say that for every pound's worth of foreign articles brought into this country ready for sane, £.3 worth was brought as the raw material to provide employment for our workers. If the tariff reformers succeeded in curtailing imports, the . exports would curtail themselves, and then where would be the more work and totter wages which they promised to the British wortman ? Colonial Preference would be a real danger and not a blessing to the Empire, and would tend to disorder and hostility instead of unity and peace. Allied with the policy of Free Trade must be that of freedom of the land. (Applause). Under the present system the power to obtain payment for the laud depended not so much on the right to use it as the right to withhold it, and as a result they had the idle rich at one end of society and ?he workless poor at the other. The land should come into the market free to the people who could put it to the best possible use.. There could be no real question until the land progress was dealt with. (Applause.) • Mr. J. Howlis referred to a speech made at a recent tariff reform meeting by Mr. G. H. Wartairton Steel who disputed the accuracy of the statements which be (Mr. Howes) made with regard to the royalties and wages paid during a voyage of the " Lusitania." Mr. Steel was a young man of some promise, who was capable of rendering good service to his party and he (Mr. tiowesi liked to enconrage thoughtful young men on either side. ' He was. however, somewhat concerned about the reputation of Mr. Steel and he wanted him to be a little more careful in some of the things he said. Mr. Steel said that Mr. Stephen Walsh, M.P. had also spoken' on the question of the "Lusitania " and that Loth Mr. Walsh and he (Mr. Howes) were inaccurate. "I did not know that Mr. Walsh had spoken on this subject "continued Mr. Howes. "It shows how great minds sometimes -think alike. (Laughter.) I am not concerned to defend Mr. Walsh—he is able to defend himself—but I believe that what he said is as strictly accurate as the retilarks. I made." Mr. Howes said his statement that the " Lusitania " constuned 16,000 tons of coal during the voyage to New York and back was based on the return given during the last run but one of that vessel. Mr. Steel then stated that he (Mr. Howes) said the coal paid in ioyalties od. per ton. If Mr. Steel would refer to the newspaper report he would find that be (Mr. Howes) did not say od. per ton but a great deal more, and he would stand by the figures as reported. Mr. Steel also credited him with stating that the total crew of the "Lusitania " numbered 333 and that the wages paid amounted to £l6O less than was paid in royalties. He said- Mr. Howes was wrong and that the number was really much greater, the crew consisting of 827 instead of 333. That was a great difference but lie (Mr. Howes) never said that the total crew numbered 333. Ile was only dealing with the stoke-hole crow—as stated in the newspaper report—and the reason was because he was referring to the royalties on the coal they burnt. Probably the owners of tho vessel had to provide the food for the crew in addition to their wages, but the point he was emphasising was that the money paid to the landlord,who did not go into the stoke-hole, for the royalties on the coal, was more than the sum paid to the wen who had to work in the stockhole. Whether the amount of royalty was as great as he (Mr. Howes) said or not, did-the tariff reformers agree that the iron industry should be crippled by the payment of royalties to people who did nothing eit•ler to put the coal into the earth or to get it out ? This was the only country in the world where the mining royalties dropped into the pockets of private individuals. The 'law which gave these mon the right to claim the minerals was made by; the landlord class before the people had any Parlia- mentary representation. When the landlords had a monopoly of both Houses they took the minerals and then passed a. law to say it was right to take them. (Laughter.) Mr. Howes concluded by reminding Mr. Steel of a letter which. the League sent a short, time ago, and to which they were still awaiting an answer. When they received it, they would have an opportunity of dealing with matters at closer quarters with the gentleman to whom the letter referred. (Applause.) Mr. S. Ll LEAVE said they did not claim that Free Trade was all that was necessary in order to secure the well-being of the masses of the people and the indus- tries of this country. We were a long way off Paradise yet, but they claimed that under Free Trade we were nearer to Paradise than we should be under tariff reform. If things were bad now,they would be worse under tariff reform. They maintained that social reform was neces- sary, but that the necessary basis for any scheme of social reform was the system of Free Trade. Before Free -Trade was introduoed, the masses of the people of this country were in a shocking state. The income of the workingman was so small and the prices of com- modities so high that every available band for whom work could ue found was set to work, and the consequence was the great prevalence of child labour. Poverty, not on the margin that we accepted now, but practically starvation was the chronic lot of the poor. He challenged anyone to deny that the number of people we were supporting on these islands and the standard of comfort were tremendously greater than they were before Free Trade came into operation. Then

ST. HELENS.

No need for coppers

If you haven't a copper, don't worry you don't need one. If you have a copper, don't light the fire. Do your washing Fels-Naptha way. Be fair to yourself. Don't do your washing in the old back-breaking way. Get a 234 d. bar of Fels-Naptha soap and try it next washing day. Try it at our expense. Your grocer will give you back your money if you're not pleased, and he'll charge the soap to us. So there !

White clothes

Wet the clothes, and soap the soiled parts with Fels-Naptha soap. Roll each piece into a close roll, and place them all in a tub with enough water to cover them. Soak for thirty minutes. Then rub in the same water; rub a little in the rinsing water; rinse in the usual way. That's all !

Fels - Naptha

The Examiner, Saturday, June 1,2 1909

LYMM WOMEN LIBERALS.

GARDEN PARTY AT LYNIM HALL.

===== 0002601_19090612_art0097.txt | included=true | included =====
Speeches by Mrs. Crossley and others.

The newly-formed Lymm Women's Liberal Associa- tion gives every promise of becoming a useful asset of the progressive forces in the division, for the member- shiP has already reached 150 and the greatest enthusiasm is being manifested in the work of the organization. On Wednesday afternoon, at the invitation of Mrs. F. H. Smith, the members were entertained at a garden party at Lymm Hall, and the function proved a gratifying success. Lymm Hall is a 'picturesque ivy-clad building,standing in beautiful grounds which are just now bright with the bloom of the rhododendron and laburnum, and the surroundings are ideal for a- garden party. Fortunately the weather, while not brilliant, kept fine and fliere were occasional spells of 6unshine. The guests, who nuin- bertd about 100, were received by Mr. and Mrs. F. H. Smith, and tea was immediately served on the lawn, the catering being in the hands of the Winmar- leigh Café Company, Warrington. Amongst those present were : Mrs. W. J. Crossley, wee of the member for the division, the Misses Smith, Mr. and Mrs. F. Milne, Mrs. Proctor, Mrs. Goto')ed (hon. secretary of the Association) and Mrs. John Brooks (hon treasurer). After tea. the visitors dispersed to inspect the trimly-kept gardens, which were greatly admired, and bt 5.30 a short meeting was held.. Mr. F. H. &Lath, who -presided„ extended a hearty welcome to the gue As. He believed that women should take a cl, ep inurest in what were real politics. Woman had a great deal to do in making the nation and she Lhould have a great deal to say in the making of the

themselves. People were saying very extravagant things now-a-days abut Mr. Lloyd-Georges Budget. Judging from what they 'aid, one would think such a terrible thing had never been done before, and many of the rich people were as mad as could be about it. The rich people could make themselves heard more than the poorer ores. Of course, the Budget did touch the ech very greatly. The other day at Windermere a gentleman said to her husband "Why have I got to pay such a lot, owing to your new Budget ?" and her husband replied" My, dear fellow, because you have got such a lot." (Laughter and applause). Mrs. Crossley also instanced the case of a Conservative lady who complained that the Budget would compel her to pay £lBO per annum more in taxes. That sounded rather dreadful until they realised that it meant that the lady had £5,000 a year to live on. (Laughter and applause). At Conservative meetings a short time since they were always shouting about Dreadnoughts.—"We want eight and we went wait" and that sort-of- sing-song. The moment these rich-people were asked to pay a little more out of their great incomes they became very quiet about Dreadnoughts. Mrs. Crossley referred to the success of the Small Holdings Act ptised by the Liberal Government. She pointed out that in 1892 the Conservatives passed some sort of Srr all Holerngs Act, but in 15 years only 889 acres of land were disposed of fur that purpose. 'I he Liberals, had in fifteen menthe, by their Small Holdings Act disposed of 21,000 acres. (Applause). Mrs. Crossley strongly advised the members to read Liberal publications and so keep themselves well posted up on the great questions of the day. She wondered if they all knew of that delightful paper pub-

MRS. W. J. CROSSLEY AT THE GARDEN PARTY,

a year ago, and the sm 11l lift-boy in some a skward way rmidentally broke her sunshade. At first he began to apologise, but his face brightened and he said: "It does not matter, maiame. You are going hack to England to-morrow and you will never necd a sunshade there." (Laughter). However, she theught they would agree that a fine and beautiful day in England heat a fine and beautiful day anywhere else. (Applause.) They were grateful and happy at being present that afternoon to get from Mr. and Mrs. Smith just the send-off and the lift-up thit they wanted, because they were a young and struggling Women's Liberia Association, although, judging from the large attendance, it hardly appeared so. The Association had not been in existence very long and it was at the very beginning that they most valued such help as Mrs. Smith had extended by entertaining them with such grace and courtesy. (Applause.) Why did they want such an association to exist at all? Because they felt growingly day by day that women wore a very important factor in politics. She vas old snough to remedtber the !ay when a great many of her own sex used to say: "I know nothing about politics and I care less." If she heard a young woman say that to-day she would think considerably less of her, becanse. it was so important that a woman should care for.the politics of her country. If they cared for their country, for their homes, and for their Empire, it was necessary that they should know on what side of politics they stood and what it was right or wrong to believe. The Conservative party had taught the Liberal party a great deal in the way of organizing their women's influence and work, and carrying it through with real. zeal. It was a case of playing that afternoon,. but she was sure the members of the Women's Liberal Association would not begrudge a little hard work in the coming autumn and winter. They could not influence other people unless they had acquired a knowledge of political sul.j(cts

That the Weaver Trustees he asked to improve the bottom portion of the lane so as to makb it suitable for pedestrians and vehicilar traffic. The minutes were approved.

THE ELECTRIC LIGHTING ORDER REVOKED.

The letter which the Clerk bad,—as-intructed by the Electrical Supply Committee, written to the Board of Trade asking them to defer consideration of the question of revoking the Newton-in-Makerfleli Electric Lighting Order, 1903, for a further period of twelve months from the 2nd May was read, . also the letter received from the Board of Trade in reply thereto, in which they stated that after careful con- sideration of all the circumstances of the case they

Dr. Watkins, medical officer, presented his quarterly report to the monthly meeting of the Newton Council on Monday, and in the course of it stated: I have the honour to submit my report for the fiast quarter of 1909. The population, esi ireated for the middle of the year (1909), is 20,714. There were registered during the quarter 150 births and 74 deaths, giving a natural increase of 76 for the quarter. Dating the quarter 94 cases were notified under the Infections Diseapes (Notification) Act, 1889. Seventeen cares of pulmonary phthisis were notified voluntarily during the quarter. The cases notified were as follows i—Diptheria, 33; erysipelas, 12 ; scarlet fever, 32; eeteric fever, 17 ; pulmonary phthisie, 17 ; total, 111.—The public health has not been so good during this quarter as.it has for a considerable time. At the beginning of the quarter the epidemic of measles, which has existed throughout the fourth quarter of last sear, was gradually dying out, but was sufficiently widespread to necessitate closure of the Manor Schools (mixed and infanta' departments) from 13th to 25th January, and the St. Peter's Infants' School from 3rd February till Ist March. Influenza followed and affected a large number of the population, but not so severely Es last year. I have no doubt that ri'large number of the deaths from bronchitis and pneumonia were due in the first place to influenza. The quarter has shown a great increase in the number of infectious diseases which has occurred, notably diphtheria, enteric fever and .scarlet fever. Full inquiries were made into each case, and in the diphtheria there was no reason to term it an epidemic. In very few instances could direct infection be traced from one case to another, and all the general causes has to be eliminated—such as milk supply, schools, &c. The disease appeared to be eudemic in the wards affected, which were notably those with a damp subsoil; not cue case occurred in those parts of the township with a sandy subsoil. With regard to the enteric fever, three of the cases were sporadic, and the source of the infection was not traceable. The whole of the rest constituted a localized epidemic. I believe that the scarlet fever is usually due no dir:ct infection; it is now usually £0 mild a dieesse that 1 am convinced cases get overlooked entirely, and are a danger to all others with whom they come in contact. With the exception of the birth and infant mortality rate., which show improvement, all are worse than piny previous quarters, notably the respiratory aud zymotic death rates and the infectious sick rate. The increase in the respiratory. death rates I believe to he due to influenza, and the increase in the zymotic death and sick rates to the enteric fever and diphtheria chiefly. The water supply has been good during the quarter.
