A Mistaken Mission.

Sir,—l was surprised on taking up the "Examiner" a May 29th to find from an extract of a sermon preached by the Rev. W. Bracecamp that the rev. gentleman is evidently very much alarmed that the Warrington battalion should be 200 behind their strength in the Territorial Army. The reason which the rev, gentleman gives for this is Simply that the young men of Warrington to-day are not doing their duty to their King and country. This prompts one .27 to ask" where is their country ?" when the majority of our English men have not as much country as would fill a decent sized flower pot, and the limit which any workingman gets is Oft. by 2ft. unless he is lucky enough before death to step in possession of a wall plot of land. And, now Sir, apart from has not the Rev. Mr. Bracecamp departed from the Gospel of our Master who was always opposed to wars and stood for peace on earth and good-will towards men? I may tell the rev, gentleman that I am not one of those persons who are either fortunate or unfortunate enough to be in a public position like himself, but.if I were it would be a long while before I would advise my fellow-country- men to -take up arms against any other country. As I read I shudder and think of the South African War, and the Chinese Labour question comes,back to me. I would ask the Rev. Mr. Bracecamp does he think that our young men are encouraged to come forward on behalf of their King and country when we see the capitalist step in and get the benefit of their self- sacrifice by the introduction of cheap labour after the war is over? Again I would say, to those who are spoiling to fight, well, let them fight to their hearts' content. They will soon get tired of it and pray for peace again if they have to go in the fighting line to be made a target of. Again I have a glance back and I see the poor widows and orphans left by the blood-stained -battle-field and ask myself the question where is their country? And now, Sir, enough has been said on this question. For God's sake, for our children's sake, and for the sake of those who are coming after us, teach the -people the way of sanity and brotherhood, and remember our Maker's words : "All ye are brothers."—Yours respectfully, A WHITE SLAVE.

THE NEW ALDERMAN.

BIOGRAPHICAL SKETCH.

A well deserved honour' has been conferred upon Councillor James Evans by his unanimous election to an aldermanic seat. For many years he has taken an active part in public affairs and there is probably no more energetic and painstaking member of the Council to-day. In response to our request for a few biographical details, Alderman Evans has supplied the following : " I was born at Frodsham Bridge on February 14th 1848, removing from Frodshani to Stockton Heath when I was seven years old. Unfortunately for me, my mother died when I was eight years of age. My lather at that time was working the old mill, known as Blinston's mill. Being one of a large family I had to start early to do something to get my own living. I became associated with the potato boats from London Bridge to Manchester, for which my only pay was just ti few coppers, which the farmers used to give me. Therefore it was to my interest to work hard. In those days the farmers used to travel" with the potato boats, and I have a very vivid recollection of the kind of sandwiches that they took with them, for what was left after lunch was given to the other boy and myself. After this I sought.

employment as a ploughboy at Crowton Hall Farm, my first year's wages being 50s. and my food. So far as my education was concerned it was practically nil. I did not think so much about food for the mind as food for the body, and so I was growing up into a young man when I came to Warrington over forty years ago. I had learned to write my own name when my friend, Mr. James Gandy, who now lives at Enfield Hall, gave me very valuable assistance in a number of lessons, and so put me in the way of help-, ing myself. By perseverance and hard work I have step by step reached a position that, in those early- days I never dreamt of. It is with pleasure I acknow- ledge the appreciation of my public life and work by the people of the good old town of my choice, and I, am still hoping that my life may be spared to faith- fully discharge such duties as May from time to time be laid upon me." It may be mentioned that on coming to Warrington Alderman Evans threw in his lot with the Independent Methodists at Friars' Green Chapel, where he has remained as a member for over forty years, filling all the offices in connection with the church and taking his place on the plan as a local preacher. Alderman Evans entered the Town Council eleven years ago as a representative of Whitecross Ward, and at the subse- quent elections he has been returned unopposed. He -represents the Corporation on the Lancashire Asylums Board and the Matchester Port Sanitary Authority and his most important office is that of Chairman of the Health Committee.- Alderman Evans is a staunch Liberal, having served as Chairman of the Executive Committee of the Liberal Association, and he has for many years been Chairman of the Liberal Committee for Whitecross Ward. For the past two years he has been a member of the Board of Guardians. CONGRATULATIONS FROM THE BOARD OF GUARDIANS. At the meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians on Wedneeday,Mr.J.Billinge said that the all- importantand august body the Town Council had con- ferred upon their friend Mr. James Evans the dis- tinguished honour of alderman. He (the speaker) was sure the members and officials of the Board would desire to offer Mr. Evans their heartiest congratula- tions. and they anticipated forhim a very joyous tenure of office. He moved that a vote of congratulation be sent. Mr. GEORGE MATHER seconded. Miss BROADBECT.—I am only sorry that Mr. Evans "hes not confer the great honour and privilege upon us—since he does us the honour of belonging to this body—of attending a little oftener. Mr. A. BROOKS said it was hardly fair to Mr. Evans to let that remark"go unchallenged. He met Mr. Evans that morning and be expressed great regret that he could not attend the meeting as he was on Town Council work. Mr. MATHER : I believe be is going to London. Mr. A. BROOKS: It is public work that, keeps him from this Board. Miss BROADBENT : lam sure he is most busy. - The motion was then heartily agreed to.

TILE ROYAL VISIT.

The local arrangements for the visit of their Majesties the King and Queen to Warrington on Tuesday, July 6th, are being rapidly pushed forward On Thursday, Lord. Derby in company with the Mayor (Alderman Forshaw), the Borough Surveyor, and the Lancashire County Surveyor, motored, along he route from Worsley to Warrington. STANDS FOR THE CHILDREN. -

The stands to Tie provided for the day school children between the ages of four and thirteen,will be allocated as follows St. Elphin's Park (where there will be two stands, one on each side of Manchester-road).—Faitfeld, St. Benedict's, Silver-street, Beamont, St. Ann's, Hamilton- street, Parochial, St. Peter's, and Blue Coat Schools— a total of 5,943 children. Howard Place.—Bolton Council, St. Mary's, Latch- ford R.C., Ladies' School of Industry and St. James's schools—a total of 2,702 children. - Liverpool-road.—St. Alban's, Arpley-street, St. Barnabas', Heathside, Holy Trinity: Sankey Bridge, Wycliffe and Sacred Heart—a total of 4,969 children The number of children accommodated on the three stands will total 13,614. The 'control of the stands and the arrangement of the children will be left to Mr. J. Moore Murray, Director of Education, and his staff. The Sub-Com- mittee recommend the Education Committee to grant the children a half-holiday on the date of the Royal visit. The scholars are to assemble on the stands not later tlom 4.30 p.m. It is suggested that a souvenir, in the form of an illuminated c ird, be presented to each child, but this will depend upon the cost.

TOWN COUNCIL AND THE LOYAL

ADDRESS.

At the monthly meeting of the Town Council on Tuesday evening, the Mayor (Alderman Forshaw) formally moved: "That a loyal address be pre- sented to their Majesties King Edward VII, and Queen Alexandra on the occasion of their Majesties passing through Warrington on the Gth July, 1909, and that the common seal of the Corporation be affixed thereto."

The DEPUTY MAToa. seconded. Alderman BOLTON supported and said they could not do better than be as loyal as any other Town Council. They should have a proper address and it should be properly presenled. Councillor TOOLE in supporting said the body to which he belonged had a very sincere regard for the King and Queen, and their Majesties might be assured that so far as lay in their power nothing would be wanting to show their loyalty and devotion to their Sovereign and his gracious Queen. The broad-minded policy with which his Majesty had identified himself had rightly earned for him the title "Edward the Peacemaker." To many of them it gave no little satisfaction to have an opportunity which those living in Warrington to-day had never previously had, of seeing presented in their good old town an address of welcome to those who were so universally beloved. (Hear, hear). Alderman BENNETT said that this was such an excep- tional occasion that it justified a word or two from him. He never thought of this country without think- ing of our other countries and without remembering ' that King Edward was the King of the greatest Empire on which the sun had ever set in the past and would ever probably shine in the future. It was, therefore, no light thing that the King should come to honour that little old town, whose history stretched back to the beginning of the Empire and which had always been so loyal and patriotic. It was, therefore, their duty to rise to the occasion, as 250- years had passed since -a King had visited the town, namely, Charles 11., but under circumstances which were not altogether auspicious. But the visit of King Edward would be auspicious, and be trusted that even the Clerk of the Weather would smile upon them. (Hear, hear.) The motion was put and carried with applau3e. TOWN CLERK'S WIG AND GOWN.

When the minutes of the General Purposes Com- mittee were submitted Councillor STEEL said that at a meeting of that committee he proposed and the Mayor seconded that on the occasion of their Majesties' visit the Council should purchase a wig and gown for the Town Clerk, and that was unanimously carried. He observed that the matter was not mentioned in the minutes. The TOWN CLERK : It was a little joke on the part of Councillor Steel, and it referred to me personally. Councillor STEEL: It was not a joke. (Laughter. Councillor PEMBERTON said that if the Town Clerk( objected, his wishes ought to be considered.

THE KNOWSLEY REVIEW.

Arrangements are practically completed for the Royal Review of Territorials in Knowsley Park on July sth._ His Majesty the King is to present colours to eight of the twelve West Lancashire battalions, seven of the colours being blessed by the Bishop of Liverpool, and the eighth (for the Scottish) by the Rev. Alexander Connell, of Sefton Park. The number of, the Territorial Forces under the command of General Bethune will be about £15,000. Generally speaking, the artillery and engineers wpill arrive by road, and the rest by rail, disentraining at Huyton and Prescot. Commencing at 4 p.m., the Review, opening with a general salute, will occupy an hour and a half, the King passing in his carriage twice along she whole line. Given fine weather, the spectacle is tare to be one of the grandest which Lancashire has ever Witnessed.

Warrington Sportsman's. Tragic End.

About noon on Thursday, Peter Lawless, a well- known --sportsman, was found dead under tragic circumstances, at his home, 110, Forster-sfreet, War. rington. A neighbour, on entering rhe house, found Lawless with a big gash in his throat, evidently self- inflicted, as a blood-stained razor was found near the body. Deceased was well-known in sporting circles in Warrington. Several years ago he played at three- quarter in the Warrington F.C.

Stockton WARRINGTON AND WIDNES BUFFALOES IN FULL REGALIA.[IIfoore, Heaih.

A friendly math at bowls between members of the Widnes R.A.0.8. and the Warrington R.A.0.8. was played on the green of the Stage Inn, Lower Walton, on Thursday, and resulted in a victory for the hom3 side. There .was a large gathering of Buffaloes from both districts, and soon after arrival the company enjoyed a walk round the Walton Hall grounds, by the courtesy of Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart. After the bowling, an excellent tea was partaken of at the Stag Inn, following which the different lodges represented paid a regalia visit to the Victoria Lodge, Stockton Heath. The photo- graph reproduced above was taken by Mr. J. V. Moore, of Stockton Heath, and shows the Warrrington and Widnes Buffaloes in their full regalia. The arrangements for the day were capably carried out by Mr. James Strange.

THE YEAR'S ASSESSMENTS.

The CLEatt reported that at the commencement of the year Mr. Meacock was ordered to value fifteen works within thc borough. The valuation of these premises at that time was gross .£18,305, rateable £14,634. The present values were gross £26,192, rateable £18,433, an increase in rateable value of gross £7,887, rateable £3,799. The rateable value of the Union at the com- mencement of the year was £588,898 gross. At the end of the year it had risen to £593,546 gross, an increase of •£,1,618.

FOR THE CHILDREN.

A Great Social Institution.

The early part of the Twentieth Century will be known at the "Children's Age." The State has awakened to the fact that children" are not wholly brain." Appetites and morals are recognised. Consequently we have free breakfasts for the pool - and a' Children's Act' which protects young life from many powerful temptations and secures for its failings justice tempered with mercy. Change was inevitable. Compulsory Education .Acts demand compulsory feeding and compulsory moral training. It has taken us a quarter of a century to realize thar-parent,tl liberty is not as sacred as a child's welfare. Adult freedom -must be limited if its possession means , morally and physically undeveloped children. Much remains to be done. Perhaps the neediest classes of juveniles have been entirely neglected, viz., the orphans and those whose case is more pitiable than the fatherless. Hitherto, unless a child has broken the law the State has shown no interest in its training. Will it take another twenty-five years before we make provision for the fatherless and motherless? Lord Rosebety greeted the International journalists with " Welcome Home " : how long must we wait before the children, whose parents have lost every trace of dignity through drink or lust, or both combined, find England a home and its Government parental in its care? Unworthy ideas of liberty have hindered our progress and kept back happiness from thousands of lives,

Where legislation has failed the Churches have triumphed; the friendless and the weak have been their special care. Oi;phan Schools and Children's Homes are recognized departments of every Church. Roman Catholicism, Protestantism and Dissent have translated loyalty to Christ into kindness to the children. Worthy citizens have been fashioned from the wreckage of society. My purpose in writing is to call' attention to one of these institutions in our neighbourhood. At Frodsham tfiere is a branch of the National Children's Home and Orphanage.. As a street collection is being taken for it I wentL'a few days ago to see for myself what is being done and to give my impressions. If anyone wonders whether it is situated on the hills or in the valley let me recommend a cycle ride. The Governor will be delighted to see you. The first thing to do is to clear the mind of the words Institution and Governor, and substitute Home and Parent: No bumbledom here : no uniforms ; no pauperism or taint of charity i Some object in our Workhouses to the inmates wearing uniform. I object to the officials wearing it. Officialism is bred through the buttons and the cut of the coat. Bumble cannot be shut out if you give a man a few brass buttons. No brass buttons at Frodsham. Every child's fancy is considered in the cut of its garment and in the shape of its hat. Just look at the wardrobe in this home and look at it—save the name—in a Workhouse. Little girl's hats all trimmed differently in the Home; •no trimming, no fitting in the House. I went to see how 150 children are fed, clothed, educated, settled in life. Two sisters have the care of 25 children in a home ; ever child its locker, its toys, its cupboards and—shade of Warrington Guardians —its toothbrush. Every month a Liverpool dentist (gratuitously) examines the teeth of the children. A local doctor prescribes for their ailments. A farmer teaches the boys farming, a shoemaker teaches them to make and repair boots. All the underclothing is made by the girls,under the guidance of instructors. "How do you manage about the religious difficulty ?" I asked. If that question was not asked the Governor might have thought I lived in another world. And so I do. "We have no religious difficulty." There are no catechisms taught. In every Home there is family prayer and Christian influences do the rest. In the Fowler Memorial Hall —a lovely building--the children were learning geography and arithmetic when I entered. As we walked from the hospital a little girl limped towards us. She seemed bright and happy. But kindness cannot undo the work of an evil . inheritance. From a brothel to a Children's Home is a great change. Alas! some of the old influences must ever remain under the changed circumstances. Another girl is going into service on Monday— fully trained and equipped. Every girl is fully trained for domestic service. Many of the boys have found a new home in Canada. Mr. J. R. Barlow, of Bolton, has visited the Canadian - Home and has reported very favourably of its work. -There are thirteen homes similar to the one at Frodsham, and during the year 2,315 children have been benefited. If you have a few hours to spare, visit the home? Mr. Walton will be pleased to shew you round his seven homes, Memorial Hall, and Hospital; whilst the twenty-one acres upon which the Home stands will provide you with beautiful walks and lovely views. Remember the children and be as generous as possible to-day. Let June 12th give gladness to many otherwise cheerless lives and inspire many noble workers amongst the friendless and the weak. T. M.

LOCAL ASSESSMENTS. IMPORTANT-A-WEALS HEARD.

Several important appeals were heard at the monthly meeting of the Warrington Union Assessment Com- mittee which was held at the Workhouse yesterday morning. Mr. J. Billinge presided and there were also present :—Messrs. A. J. Jolley, G. Mather, W. Gill, A. Brooks, W. Furness, J. Evans, R. Bolton, R. Forshaw, A. Bottomley (clerk), R. R. Meacock (valuer), W. H. Wood- cock (assistant overseer for Warrington), C. Cole (assistant overseer for Newton) and J. Dickenson (assistant overseer for Haydock). MESSRS. ARMITAGE AND RIGBY, LIMITED. Mr. S. R. ARMITA GE, on behalf of Messrs. Armitage and Rigby Ltd., appealed against the assessrcent of their cotton mill in Orford-street at £5,575 gross and £3,575 rateable. He said that in 1906 the assessment was advanced from £2,400 to £2,688, the advance being made on an enlargement which had been made. In 1908 the figure was again advanced to £2,723 in respect of some small additions which had been carried out. Since that period they bad made extensions, but nothing much. The mhole of the extensions they had • made, if they took them. on what they understood to be the rateable way of assessing, would not come to any- thing like the increase which had now been made in the valuation. They assumed that the old rating was con- sidered to be fair and they were prepared to show all the monies they had since spent, and they considered it was right and proper that the increased assessment should be made on the amounts which had been expended since the last assessment. They considered they were very much over-rated, if the amount was arrived at on the additions to the works. The whole matter was one which they did not understand, unless it be that the whole of the premises had been re-valued on a different scale. The CHAIRMAN: The probabilities are that during this period you have revolutionised the interior of the works, bi ought, them up-to-date by new machinery and so forth sshich has trade them more valuable. You twentieth century businessmen-must be up-to-date and probably you have spent a large amount on new machinery which has given an enhanced letting valtie to the mill, provided you wished to let it in the open market. •,, Mr. ARMITA GE : We have kept up-to-date with, the machinery, certainly, but I have yet to understand that you are rating machery. The previous valuations have been on the buildings only, and • there has not been any assessment on machinery in any town that I know' of. The CHAIRMAN: This is an assessment upon the whole of the works. Mr. ARMITAGE : Am I to understand that it is a re-valuation of the works which have been valued before? The CHAIRMAN: It is a re-valuation of the whole works. The professional valuer considers the figures,if anything, to be to your advantage. I gather you are not aggrieved at the figures. You think they are reasonable? Mr. Artimmos: : No. We don't think they are reasonable at all. ,We think they are excessive. I think it is very much to the detriment of the Corporation that assessments should be enhanced against manu- facturers. The town lives on manufactures. The household and shop property would materially suffer if extinction of the works took place on account of valustion. I don't say it has arrived at that yet, hut we are drifting that way. We keep going very steadily and employ 2,000 people, and I think we ought to be looked upon in a lenient spirit. It was decided that the 'Valuer and the Clerk should confer with the firm on the matter. RYLANDS BROS. RECREATION CLUB::'

Mr. H. H. FIELD appealed on behalf of Rylands Bros. Workmen's Recreation Club against the assessment of their pavilion and grounds near Gorsey-lane at £270, gross, £230 rateable. He said the rental of the ground was only £36 103. per pear. The cost of the pavilion, which was not of a permanent character, was £2,813. The club was not being run for a profit, and the grounds might really be considered in the light of a public park, because all the men and boys employed by the firm, to the number of about 1500, could make use of them, and it was an effort of the management of Messrs. Rrlands Bros. to provide suitable recreation. The club was, however, purely and simply a workmen's undertaking, and he thought it was a movement that ought to be encouraged. P In answer to Mr. Evans,i Mr. FIELD slid a consider- able sum had been spent n laying out the land, but be did not think that improved its valuefor purposes other than that for which it was at present held. The area was nine or ten acres- and it was undeveloped land before the club took it over. They had laid three bowling greens and the cost, including labour, seed, &c., would be about £5O per green. Any profit that might be made would be devoted to extensions, the projects which they had in view including a gymnasium and a miniature rifle range. The club was open all the year round, but there was only a small attendance in the winter months. Mr. FORSTIANV said that estimating the income sit between £5OO and £6OO, the profit would probably come to £3OO. Mr. FIELD: No, I don't think so. I should be very glad if we made that much. Continuing he said that Mr. Rylands, Aho was one of the trustees, felt very strongly that the figures were outrageous. The Committee declined to make any change in the assessment, the Chairman remarking that if the club were a proprietory business the figures would be con- siderably larger. Mr. FIELD: I cannot understood how anybody in this room would give £3OO a year for the place and try to make a living out of it. I think it might be arranged for someone to take it over. THE INFIRMARY. Mr. J. H. J. HAMPSON, secretary ofithe Infirmary and Dispensary, appealed against the assessment of that institution at .E 250 gross £212 rateable. He said there were in the Infirmary 71 beds and 12 cots, making a total accommodation for patients of 83. In addition, there was the top ward in the new pavilion, with room for 22 beds, but this was empty. The number of beds occupied to-day was 63, leaving 20 beds empty, owing to lack of funds. During the year ended 1908, 753 in-patients and 8774 home and out-patients and accidents were treated at a cost of £4,604. The income received during the year amounted to £3,403 leaving a deficit on that year's working of £l,2ol,which together with the debit balance of £671 at the end of December, 1907, made a total debit on maintenance account. on January Ist, 1903, of £1,872. This year they had opened another small Ward, at an estimated increased annual expenditure of £5OO. Front January Ist to date slightly over £3OO had been received in new and increased annual subscriptions. The Board had placed a contract for an electric lift at a cost of £l5l. With these additions it was estimated that the debit balance at the close of this year would amount to £3272. As the Committee were kind enough to make a reduction in the assessment when the deficit stood at £671, his Beard felt sure that with a present deficit of nearly three times that amount, and a likely deficit at the close of the year of nearly five times as large,they might without seeming too importunate respectfully request the Committee to make a corresponding decrease in the amount of the assessment. He would suggest for their consideration the fact that by the expenditure of this large sum of money every year the Infirmary authorities saved the rates of the Union to a very material extent for it was clear that many of the injured and sick poor would, but for being returned to the ranks of effective citizenship and workers by the help the Infirmary extended, either gravitate to the Workhouse or

A fashionable wedding of considerable local interest took place at St. James' Church, Picadilly, London,W., on Wednesday, when Mr. Tom Airey, son of Mr. George-Airey, of Sandfield Hall, Newton-le-Willows, was married to Miss Moria Elena Kennard-Davis, daughter of Mr. Ernest E. Kennard-Davis, of 38, Hyde Park Gate, London, S.W. A large congregation assembled to witness the ceremony. The officiating clergy were the Rev. J. B. Hunt, 8.D., and the Rev. Canon McCormick, D.D. The service was fully choral and included the hymns "The voice that breathed o'er Eden," "0 perfect Love "and " Now thank we all our God" and the anthem solo "Be thou faithful unto death." Mr. Stanley Marchant presided at the organ and played appropriate selections, including Mendelssohn's " Wedding March." The bride, who was given away by her father, was attired in a beautiful wedding dress of ivory satin charmeuse very, simply made, with long train forming part of the bodice which was outlined with pearls and silk embroidery. The under bodice and sleeves were embroidered in ninon. A tulle veil was worn over a coronet of orange blossoms, myrtle and white heather, the gift of the bridegroom. The only ornament was a string of pearls, the gift of the bridegroom's mother. The bridesmaids were the Misses Ines and Santa Kennard- Davis (sisters of the bride), Miss Lettie Airey (sister of the bridegroom), Miss Marie Kiely and Miss Hilda Law. Their handsome gowns were in soft parma violet ninon-de-soie over pastel blue satin, with hand embroidery in peculiar Eastern design uniting the two colours of the dress with slight introduction of plantinum. They wore large hats of plentimun shaded straw with roses in the colours of the dress round the crown and carried bouquets of pale pink roses tied with plantinum coloured streamers. Their ornaments were gold and plantinum curb bracelets which, with the bouquets, were the gifts of the bride- groom. Mr. R. Hopwood Airey, (brother of the bridegroom) discharged the duties of best man, while Messrs. E. W. Kennard Davis, C. A. Dottridge, H. A. Furniss and W. Shaw acted as groomsmen. After the ceremony, a reception was held at 38, Hyde Park Gate, the residence of the bride's parents. Later in the day the happy couple left for the Lakes of Killarney where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride's travelling dress was of soft silk voile over satin of a dare de June hue embroidered by hand in qUaint Egyptian design intermixed with platinum. En suite was worn a cloak made entirely of satin and lace in alternate rows with a belt of aluminium and a large Tuscan and black straw hat with rose at side. The wedding gown, bridesmaids' costumes and travelling dress were designed by Harrod's, London, The bride and bridegroom were the recipients of many handsome presents.

houses which the low wage-evning section occupied were closed, the people must go somewhere else, and as a consequence they found two families living in one home and the condition of things was more congested than before. The Health Committ-e were moving slowly, and they did not feel justified in rushiue, because they kuew what trouble would follow. With regard to the sub-committee appointed to report on the mit ary questiou,information was being obtained in order to enable them to carry out their task. There wore many causes of infantile mortality. Dr. Joseph had referred to one and he (Alderman Evans) hoped thit what he had suggested to the Council would soon be an accomplished fact. The minutes were then approved.

S WARRINGTON 3 CHAPTERS . BRIDGE-STREET SIXTY YEARS AGO. In a recent issue we printed a list of the shopkeepers who plied their trades on the west side of Bridge-street half a century ago. It has suggested itself to us as an interesting task to set down the names of all the old business firms of the town and secure where possible any remembrances of our oldest inhabitants. We are fortunate in having, in Mr. Joseph Clare a Warringtonian whose memory of old Bridge-street is singularly complete, and we have drawn upon his stores of information for a list of shop keepers on the east side of the street and print his comments thereupon. Commencing our walk at the Bridge there stood first the grocer's shop kept by Thorp and afterwards by Burgess. The Packet House stood as it does now at the corner of Mersey- street, and as the packets departed from the wood pier opposite it was a packet house in more than name. It was then kept by a Scotchman named Blythe. The old wood stage from which the packets departed has of course disappeared, the only relic of it being the long wood post which rises from the mud of the river. Mr. FairhurSt's, grocer's shop, stood next to the Packet House, and the next establishment was a drapery shop kept by another Scotchman, McGowan. The shop now occupied by Mr. Birchall, was kept by Halliwell, the well-known clock- maker. Samples of his handiwork in the shape of fine trustworthy grandfather's clocks may still be found in Warrington homes. Another ingenious student of timepieces, Mr..Litherland, lived in Latchford in a shop that stood on the site occupied by Hallwoods, and between the two a close friendship existed. Mr. Litherland is known as the inventor of the lever watch, and it is due to the memory of Mr. Halliwell to say that he gave valuable assistance to his friend in the long extensive studies and experiments which preceded the advent of the first lever watch. The next shop was that of Miss Ely, bonnet maker, who married James Forrest, and in the shop now occupied by Pierpoints, Peter Bate carried on the business of confectioner. Next was the chemist's shop of Dr. Peter Sadler. As shopman, Mr. Goodall did duty for many years and by his diligence in business made it possible for • his young employer to qualify as a medical man. The Bull's Head came next, -.lld was then kept by Mather, and adjoining it the Seven Stars kept by Boardman. The watch shop of William Simcock adjoined the Seven Stars, and the next was Crank, cabinet maker; then came a shop shared by the father and brother of William Silcock the fathet being a watchmaker, and the son a barber--a curious combination but not regarded as anything unusual in those days. The "Royal Oak" flourished then as now, its keeper then being named Bolton. Next was the grocer's shop now kept by Mr. Hooley. The "Higher Seven Star's" stood next, and was a great resort of the factory hands of Latchford. The innkeeper was Pete. Price. Mr. Rowe's book shop, Mrs. Finch, and Wilkinson, both provision dealers, were the next three and then came Cavanagh's picture shop. This last is now the "Rose and Crown " Inn, but the "Rose and Crown" of those days was approached from the passage to which it gave its name and had no frontage on Bridge-street. The innkeeper was Mr. Stanton and he ran a daily coach to Manchester. On the other side of the Rose and Crown passage was Dutton's, the emporium for fine drapery and forerunner of the present shop in Sankey Street. Next there was the private house of Mr. Davies, father of the late Robert Davies, solicitor. On the site now occupied by Mr. Pearson's shop stood Mr. Davies's office. The business carried on was that of wine merchant, but Mr. Davies was a man of many interests, and carried on -in addition the business of oil-sheet maker. He had also the saw mills near what is now Arpley Station, and conducted also a rope making business. , The old house iri which he lived still stands but with amodern shop front ; it is occupied by Mr. Harold Smith, chemist. Randal Podmore, barber, had the next shop and next to him was Walsh the tin worker, whose workshop was in Ship-yard. The " Ship " Inn and Ship-yard were next reached. The" Ship" in those days faced upon Bridge-street, and stood back a little from the line of shops. In the space thus left Peter Phillips used to display a row of chairs, samples of his handiwork, on market days. The Red Lion Hotel, than, as now, prominent among the hostelries of tile town. It was kept by Mr. Holland and afterwards by Mr. Fred Thorpe. In Chrimes yard there stood another public-house. This was kept by a quaker, Thomas Leadbetter, whom we should imagine to be the last quaker to hold the position of inn-keeper in Warrington— possibly in England. Boardman's barber's shop came next, then Carter's grocer's shop. An irohmonger's shop occupied the site of Garlands, its pro- prietor being Mr. Heath, a well known townsman. Then came The Star and Garter, now Ashurst'S,and adjoining it was Pickmere the wine merchant's the only name ivhich still remains in the line of shopkeepers of 50 years ago. A tailor named O'Kell occupied the old tailoring establishment to which Boydell's have succeeded and Smith's tin shop came next to it. Sanderson, the grocer was the predecessor of Richardson and the shop occupied the site. Next wal Mallet's hat and cap shop, its occupier living in Paragon-place. After Cartwright's pro- vender shop (next to Dolman's-lane) ,kr.as Webster the chemist. The site of the National Bank was occupied by David Green, draper, and next came Carter's who combined the businesses of watchmaker and crockery dealer. Mr. Shaw was butcher, and came next in the line of shops, and next to him was Bethel's glassware depot, and then Bostock's shoe shop (now Bailey's). Messrs. Watts, of Manchester, so well known in the business world, occupied the fine shop which now serves Mr. Atkinson for a similar business. Mr. George Blackhurst, watch maker, held the next shop, and then came George Hatton, butcher. Next were Topping and Sharp, chemists (now Boots) and William Milner, tailor. The corner shop at Market Gate was the grocery store of Peter Smith whose house, approached from Buttermarket-street, stood behind it. In the passages leading from Bridge-street at the north end we need hardly say there were several interesting old Inns that flourished in the coaching days. In Dolman's-lane there was the public-house known as the "Bang up "—whatever the name they have meant. In the passage next to Atkinson's was the old Swan—formerly the "Two necked Swan" kept by Maxwell. The next passage gave access to the "Cock". and the next to the " Crow " which was then kept by Thomas Allen. A.M.
